v PREFACE  
This Report of the Comptroller and Auditor General (CAG) of India on Public Health 
Infrastructure and Management of Health Services for the period  ended March 2022 
has been prepared for submission to Lieutenant Governor of Jammu and Kashmir 
under Section 72 of the Jammu and Kashmir Reorganisation Act 2019 for laying in 
the Legislative Assembly .  
This Report contains IX chapters .  
Chapter I  contains the organisational setup  and brief introduction about the Public 
Healthcare facilities in the UT of Jammu and Kashmir.  
Chapter II contains the audit findings pertaining to  availability of Human Resource s 
in the Health  and Medical Education  Department . 
Chapter III contains the audit observations on availability of Healthcare services  in 
Jammu and Kashmir  (Outpatient Department and In -patient Department)  
Chapter IV contains the audit observations relating to availability of drugs, 
medicines, equipment and other consumables . 
Chapter V contains the audit observations pertaining to availability of health care 
infrastructure in J&K.  
Chapter V I contains significant audit findings relating to budgetary outlay  and 
expenditure of the Health  and Medical Education Department . 
Chapter VII deals with the audit findings pertaining to implementation of Centrally 
Sponsored Schemes in J&K.  
Chapter VIII contains  the significant findings pertaining to adequacy and 
effectiveness of Regulatory Mechanisms.  
Chapter IX deals with the Sustainable Development Goal 3 and its adoption in J&K.  
The instances mentioned in this Report are those which came to notice during  
test-check of records of 2016 -17 to 2020-21. Instances relating to the period 
subsequent to March 2021  have also be en included, wherever necessary . 
The A udit has been conducted in conformity with the Auditing Standards issued by 
the Comptroller and Auditor General of India.  

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  EXECUTIVE SUMMARY  

 
  

vii EXECUTIVE SUMMARY  
National Health Policy (NHP), 2017 was adopted to strengthen and prioritise the role 
of the Government in shaping health system in all its dimensions.  Considering the 
goals laid down in NHP  2017 and experience in COVID -19 pandemic, the 
Performance Audit (PA) on “Public Health Infrastructure and Management of Health 
Services” was conducted  to assess the adequacy of financial resources allocated, 
availability of health infrastructure, manpower, machinery and equipment in the 
Health Institutions  (HIs)  as well as efficacy in the management of health services in 
the State. The Performance Audit also  covers the efficacy of the regulatory framework 
being enforced by the Government to regulate public/ private  health sector, schemes 
being implemented by Government of India through State Government and overall 
linkage with the Sustainable Development Goal -3. The audit was conducted for the 
period 2016 -21 but wherever feasible, the data has been updated up to 2021 -22 or 
later.  
The Ministry of Health and Family Welfare, Government of India, has issued  (first in 
2007 and revised in 2012 and 2022)  a set of uniform standards called the Indian 
Public Health Standards (IPHS) to improve the quality of healthcare delivery in the 
country and serve as a benchmark for assessing performance of healthcare delivery 
system. The IPHS norms for District Hospitals (DHs), Sub -District Hospitals (SDHs), 
Community Health Centres (CHCs), Primary Health Centres (PHCs) and Sub -Centres 
(SCs) prescribe standards for the services, manpower, equipment, drug s, building and 
other facilities. These include the standards to bring the health institutions to a 
minimum acceptable functional grade (indicated as essential) with scope for further 
improvement (indicated as desired).  
In addition to IPHS, various standards and guidelines on healthcare services issued by 
Government of India such as the Assessor’s Guidebook for Quality Assurance;  
Bio-Medical Waste Management Rules; and Drugs and Cosmetic Rules were used to 
evaluate the healthcare facilities in health institutions.   
As per National Family Health Survey (2019 -21) and S ample Registration System 
(SRS)  Bulletin 2020, the health indicators in Jammu and Kashmir (J&K) in respect of 
Infant Mortality Rate  (16.3 against national average of 35.2) , Neonatal Mortality Rate 
(9.8 against national average of 24.9 per 1,000 live births), death rate  (4.6 against 
national average of 6 per 1000 population)  and girl children born  (976 against national 
average of 929)  for every 1,000 boys were better than the national average .   
However, for health indicators like mothers whose last birth was protected against 
neonatal tetanus,  mothers who consumed iron folic acid tablets for 100 days   
(29.8 per cent  against national average of 44.1 per cent )  and 180 day s (15.9 per cent  
against national average of 26 per cent ), average out -of-pocket expenditure per 
delivery in a public health facility  (` 5,145 against national average of ` 2,916)  births 
delivered by Caesarean section in public  (42.7 per cent  against national average of 
14.3 per cent ) and private (82.1 per cent  against national average of 47.4 per cent)  

Audit Report on Public Health Infrastructure & Management of Health Services for the period  ended March 2022  
viii health facilities and sex ratio of the total population (females per 1,000 males)  the 
national indicators were better than UT .  
In respect of Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), the  UT a dopted 3 4 indicators 
out of 41 indicators  at the national level; covering  12 targets at UT level out of  
13 targets at national level.   
As far as Ayushman Bharat (AB) is concerned, we have included findings related to 
Health & Wellness Centres (HWC) and have also considered implementation of AB 
while making recommendations in various areas of health sector.  
There were persistent shortages in all the cadres in the health institutions. Government 
had not ensured availability of posts in the health sector as the sanctioned posts were 
not in keeping with the population to be served, affecting the health services 
adversely. This shortage was quite high in several key posts such as doctors, staff 
nurses, paramedical and other staff  who play a very important role in delivering 
comprehensive healthcare to the beneficiaries. Available manpower including the 
crucial manpower has not been distributed uniformly across the districts. Against the 
sanctioned strength of 46,115  posts, the UT of J&K had 30,745 (67  per cent ) doctors, 
nurses and paramedical staff as of March 2022. One government doctor was serving a 
population of 2,191  (2,461 in case of allopathic doctors)  in the UT  of J&K . The ratio 
of availability of doctor was better than the States of Haryana ( 4,081 doctors for  
2.51 crore people i.e. one allopathic doctor per 6,212 persons) and Punjab  
(one allopathic doctor for 6,390 persons).   
There was skewed distribution of manpower as s hortfall of overall manpower was 
lowest (11.52 per cent ) in Kulgam District and highest (43.55 per cent ) in Kishtwar 
District.  
There was a shortage  of specialists in Health and Medical Education Department 
(H&MED). Shortage of manpower in District Hospitals (DHs)  had affected delivery 
of medical services resulting in either the patients having to wait in queues outside 
the OPDs for a long  time or compelling the patients to approach other hospitals 
having such facility.  
Under the National Health Mission (NHM), shortage of doctors, paramedical staff and 
nurses in the UT of J&K was respectively 19 per cent,  08 per cent and 16  per cent in 
2021 -22. In the six sampled districts,  although most  of the emergency services were 
available in all the sampled District Hospitals except DH, Handwara, availability of 
Emergency Operation Theatre was not available in seven  SDHs  (out of  
18 test -checked) . Blood storage unit was not available in 10 SDHs  (out of  
18 test -checked ) and nine CHCs  (out of 10 test -checked) . 
The Out patient Department (OPD) services of ENT, Psychiatry, Ophthalmology and 
Skin & VD were not available in eight, nine, six and 13 DHs respectively. Moreover , 
all the required essential OPD services were not available in all 18 test -checked  
Sub-District Hospitals (SDHs) and all OPD services as required as per IPHS norms 

Executive Summary  
ix were not available in the 10 test -checked Community Health Centr es (CHCs). 
Registration  counters were also not found adequate in Tertiary Level Hospitals 
(TLHs) and District Hospitals ( DHs).  
All In-patient Department ( IPD) services as per IPHS 2012 norms were available in 
only three  DHs out of six test-checked DHs.  In respect of 28 sampled SDHs/  CHCs, 
all IPD services were available in only seven  SDHs and one CHC. OT services were 
available in all test -checked DHs. Out of sampled 18 SDHs,  OT facility was available 
in 11 SDHs . Out of 52 sampled PHCs, only two PHCs (Majalta and Achabal) had OT 
facility for surgical procedures such as Vasectomy, Tubectomy and Hydrocelectomy.  
Ambulance service was available in all test -checked DHs and SDHs/  CHCs except 
SDH Langate. However, ambulance service was available in only 19 out of  
52 sampled PHCs.  
The Discharge Rate was more than 90 per cent  in five out of six sampled DHs  
(except DH Ramban) which indicated that the hospitals are providing healthcare 
facility to the patients efficiently.  The referral out rate in respect of five out of six 
sampled DHs was less than 10 per cent whereas the referral out rate in respect of DH 
Ramban was higher as compared to other HIs. It was also observed in case of DH 
Ramban that there was shortage of doctors, non -availability of essential drugs and 
poor availability of equipment.  
Audit assessed availability of drugs against essential drugs list (EDL)  as notified by 
the H&MED and equipment listed in IPHS norms.  There  was shortage of essential 
drugs and equipment in all test-checked HIs. Availability of the prescribed essential 
equipment was also found deficient in test -checked institutions. There was d elay in 
finalisation of rate contract s that would have had adverse impact on timely supply of 
drugs/  equipment to the health institutions. There was delay in empanelment of 
laboratories for testing and analysis of drugs and in furnishing of test/analysis reports 
by empanelled laboratories. Non -implementation of e -Aushadhi / Drug and Vaccine 
Distribution Management System  for the entire chain of drug supply was also 
observed.  
Estimation, planning and creation of requisite infrastructure facilities are essential for 
ensuring the provision of optimum level of healthcare facilities.  There was inadequate 
availability of Sub -Centres (SCs) and CHCs in the UT of J&K.  In the six sampled 
Districts, there was shortage of SCs (45 per cent ) and CHCs (81 per cent ).  
Although  the r equisite number of Health and Wellness Centr es (HWCs ) have been 
upgraded , there was shortage of infrastructure, availabilit y of services, human 
resources, diagnostic services  etc. in the HWCs in the six selected D istricts. Out of 
415 construction works taken up for execution under N ational Health Mission, Prime 
Minister’s Development Programme ( PMDP ) and Languishing  Projects programme , 
177 works were completed  and 238 works remained incomplete . Works  under District 
Plan, National Bank for Agriculture and Rural Development (NABARD) and Border 
Area Development Programme (BADP) remained incomplete either due to dispute or 

Audit Report on Public Health Infrastructure & Management of Health Services for the period  ended March 2022  
x non-execution  of balance works. Out of sampled 10 CHCs , six CHCs did not have 
beds as per norms. Out of 18 sampled SDHs, 11 SDHs had less than 31 beds. Out of 
52 selected Primary Health Centres  (PHCs), seven PHCs were functioning without 
beds and 33 PHCs were lacking required number of beds as per Indian Public Health 
Standards (IPHS) 2012.  
Total expenditure of GoJ&K during 2016 -17 to 2021 -22 was ` 3,60,198.78 crore 
while expenditure on the health sector was ` 25,127.63 crore against budget allotment 
of ` 30,513.98 crore.  Expenditure on Health sector vis-a-vis total expenditure of the 
erstwhile State/  UT Government had increased from 5.84 per cent  in 2016 -17 to  
7.98 per cent  in 2021 -2022.  Expenditure on the health sector accounted for  
6.98 per cent  of the total expenditure of the erstwhile State/  UT for the period  
2016 -17 to  2021 -22. The expenditure on Health sector to erstwhile State/ UT GSDP 
had increased from 2.25 per cent  in 2016 -17 to 2.95 per cent  in 2020 -21 but 
decreased to 2.80 per cent  during 2021 -22. Further , the shortfall in utilisation of 
allotted funds rang ed between three per cent and 26 per cent  during the period  
2016 -22. 
The objective of Janani Suraksha Yojana  (JSY) under NHM was to reduce the 
maternal and infant mortality rate. The percentage of institutional deliveries remained 
in the range of 92 per cent  and 95 per cent  during 2016 -2022 . During 2016 -22, cash 
incentive s under JSY  were  provided to beneficiaries ranging between 56  per cent and  
73 per cent  of institutional deliveries  in erstwhile State/  UT of J&K . However, in six 
selected D istricts cash incentives were  provided to 97 per cent beneficiaries . Maternal 
mortality and infant mortality had decreased from 132 to 84 and 2 ,288 to  
2,108 respectively during 2016 -22. 
In the six sampled Districts, maternal deaths declined from 31 to 23 during 2016 -21 
and again increased to 31 during 2021 -22, whereas infant deaths declined from 234 to 
76 during 2016 -22. 
Under Janani Shishu Suraksha Karyakram (JSSK), an initiative to assure free services 
to all pregnant women and sick new -borns accessing public health institutions , 
achievements in respect of targets for deliveries to be benefitted declined from  
90 per cent  to 77 per cent during 2016 -21. The achievement increased to 95 per cent  
in 2021 -22.  
The achievement against the targets in respect of sick new -borns to be treated was 
more than the targets fixed in four years (2017 -18 to 2019 -20 and 2021 -22).  
Against target of 13,49,948 doses each for BCG, OPV, PV, IPV, Hep -B, DPT -B, and 
Measles, the achievement had ranged between 71 per cent and 94 per cent  whereas 
the achievement in respect of DPT -5, TT -10 and TT -16 was 64, 56 and 40 per cent  
respectively during 2016 -22 in the erstwhile State/  UTof J&K.  
There  was lack of  an adequate  mechanism whereunder drug samples were to be 
referred to State Drug s Controller (SDC) for testing on regular basis. For registration 

Executive Summary  
xi and regulation of the Clinical Establishments (CEs), no survey was conducted to 
ascertain the actual number of CEs eligible for registration. As a result, the possibility 
of CEs operating without registration could not be ruled out. There was disparity in 
rates of various pathological/radiological tests. Requisite annual reports were not 
submitted by some of the Health Care Facilities (HCFs) under Bio -Medical Waste 
(BMW) Management  Rules, 2016  and authorisation was not obtained by the occupier 
or operator handling BMW  in all cases . 
Recommendations : 
In view of the above mentioned findings, Audit recommends the following:  
The Government should consider revising the sanctioned strength of Health 
Department at par with the IPHS norms  and posting of staff in health institutions at 
par with the sanctioned strength in the primary, secondary and tertiary he althcare 
institutions. Government should bring out a long -term strategy and policy to reduce 
variations in doctor -population ratio across districts.   
Government should ensure availability of all OPD services, IPD services, emergency 
services, OT services, maternity services and ICU services in all health institutions 
and basic diagnostic services at se condary level health facilities as prescribed under 
IPHS norms. Steps should be taken to improve and strengthen auxiliary and support 
services to improve overall healthcare experience.  
Availability of essential drugs and equipment should be ensured at all health 
institutions. Delay in finalisation of rate contracts should be avoided to ensure timely 
supply of drugs/  equipment to the health institutions. e -Aushadhi/  Distribution 
Management System ( DVDMS) portal shou ld be utilised optimally by end users for 
maintenance of stocks and distribution of items  to their sub-stores  and to the patients.  
The Government may look into the issues of delays in commencement and 
completion of planned infrastructural works with a view to remov ing bottlenecks and 
ensure speedy completion.  
Government may consider utilising the allocated budget to ensure availability of 
ample and quality healthcare infrastructure and services to the people. Government 
may release funds to the State Health Society well in time for effective utilisation of 
funds in programme implementation.   
Government may consider disbursement of incentive to all the beneficiaries under 
Janani Suraksha Yojana (JSY) and providing adequate essential drugs and 
consumables under Janani Shishu Suraksha Karyakram (JSSK) for effective 
implementation of the schemes. Government may also attempt to increase awareness 
and outr each through various activities  for making the target population aware of the 
benefits of sterilisation and spacing methods.   
The targeted number of inspections may be carried out to ensure  quality of the drugs 
and drug samples may be referred to State Drug Controller for testing on regular 

Audit Report on Public Health Infrastructure & Management of Health Services for the period  ended March 2022  
xii basis. Government may ensure that all utilities generating Bio-Medical  waste comply 
with the provisio ns with regard to authorisation and submission of annual returns . 

 
  
CHAPTER -I 
INTRODUCTION  

 
  

 
1 CHAPTER -I 
Introduction  
The health  sector is one of the important social s ectors , which is directly concerned 
with the welfare of the populace and is the barometer of the structural and technological 
developme nts/ improvements of any State/  Union Territory. Access to preventive, 
promotive and curative healthcare services is a basic right of the people.  
National Health Policy, 2017  consists of Specific Quantitative Goals and Objectives 
outlined under three broad components viz. (a) Health Status and Programme Impact 
(b) H ealth Systems Performance and (c) Health S ystem s Strengthening. These goals are 
aligned  to achieve sustainable development in the health sector in keeping with the 
policy thrust.  
1.1 Public healthcare facilities in the UT  of Jammu and Kashmir  
India's p ublic health system has been  developed over the years as a three -tier system,  
namely primary, secondary, and tertia ry level of health care. Sub -Centres (SCs) and 
Primary Health Centres (PHCs) including New Type Primary Health Centres1 
(NTPHCs) and Urban Primary Health Centres (UPHCs) are primary level healthcare 
units which provide initial healthcare services to the people. Patients requiring more 
serious health care attention are referred t o the second tier of the health care system 
consisting of Commu nity Health Centres (CHCs), Sub -District Hospitals (SDHs) and 
District Hospitals  (DHs)  established in each district for providing preventive, 
promotive and curative healthcare services. A Tertiary Hospital is a hospital that 
provides tertiary care, which is healthcare from specialists in a large  hospital  after 
referral from primary  care and secondary care. Tertiary health care services are provided 
by the hospitals associat ed with the Government Medical C olleges.  All the three levels 
of primary, secondary and tertiary healthcare services in the UT are administered by 
the Department of Health and Medical Educ ation , Jammu and Kashmir.  
Indian Public Health Standards (IPHS) are a set of uniform standards envisaged to 
improve the quality of healthcare delivery in the country. IPHS norms were revised in 
2012 and 2022 keeping in view the changing protocols of the existing programmes and 
introduction of new programmes, especially for Non -Communicable Diseases . The UT 
Government of Jammu and Kashmir has not adopted IPHS norms  so far.

4 1.4 Status of Health Indicators in the UT of J&K  
The healthcare services in a State/UT can be evaluated on the basis of the achievement 
against the benchmark of health indicators . Data of health indicators at  an all-India level 
was depicted in the National Family Health Survey (NFHS -5) for the period 2019 -21 
whereas data of health indicators for Jammu and Kashmir was included in the said 
survey for the year 2019 -20. The status of some important health indicators of UT of 
J&K vis-a-vis the national average and National Health Policy (NHP) -2017 goals is 
given in Chart 1.2 .

(i) In NFHS -5 (2019 -21), the performance of J&K was better than the National average 
for parameters like Under -five mortality rate (U5MR), mothers who had an antenatal 
check -up in the first trimester and mothers who had at least 4 antenatal care visits.   
The UT also featured better in the areas of institutional births, institutional births in 
public facility and births attended by skilled health personnel.  
However, in the following areas the UT was below the national level: Sex ratio of the 
total population (females per 1,000 males) was 1 ,020 at the national level whereas in 
J&K, it was 948. Mothers whose last birth was protected against neonatal tetanus was 
marginally below the national level. Similarly, mothers who consumed iron folic acid 
tablets for 100 days and 180 days were also below the nation al level. Average out -of-
pocket expenditure per delivery in a public health facility in J&K was higher than th e 
national average. Moreover, births delivered by Caesarean section in public and private 
health facilities were  higher than the national level.  
(ii)  Performance of J&K in NFHS -5 (2019 -20) in comparison to the previous survey, 
i.e. NFHS -4 (2015 -16) improved for parameters like Under -five mortality rate 
(U5MR), mothers who received antenatal check -up in the first trimester, institutional 
births, births attended by skilled health personnel. The performance also deteriorated    
on many health indicators such as sex ratio of the total population (females per 1 ,000 
males) , mothers who consumed iron folic acid for 100 days and 180 days or more when 
they were pregnant also decreased marginally. Further, the average out -of-pocket 
expenditure per delivery in a public health facility has risen from ` 4,225 to ` 5,145 
while the number of births delivered by Caesarean section in public as well as private 
health facilities has increased .

Audit Report on Public Health Infrastructure & Management of Health Services for the period  ended March 2022  
8 1.6 Audit Objectives  
The National Health Policy (NHP)  2017  builds on the progress made in 14 years since 
the last NHP came out in 2002. The context had changed in four major ways:  
➢ First, although maternal and child mortality have rapidly declined , there is a 
 growing burden on account of non -communicable diseases and some infectious 
 diseases.  
➢ The second important change is t he emergence of a robust health care industry 
 estimated to be growing at double digit.  
➢ The third change is the growing incidences of catastr ophic expenditure due to 
 health care costs, which are presently estimated to be one of the major 
 contributors to poverty.  
➢ Fourth, rising economic growth enables enhanced fiscal capacity.  
Therefore , National Health Policy (NHP ) was adopted to respond to these contextual 
changes. The primary aim of NHP 2017 is to inform, clarify, strengthen and prioritise 
the role of the Government in shaping health systems in all its dimensions.  
Considering the goals laid down in NHP 2017 and experience in COVID -19 pandemic, 
it has become crucial to assess the adequacy of financial resources, availability of health 
infrastructure, manpower, machinery and equipment in the health institutions, as we ll 
as efficacy in the management of health services in the Union Territory (UT) , through 
existing policy interventions and look at the scope for further improvement.   
Thus, to ensure timely and systematic corrections, a Performance Audit (PA) on the 
Public Health Infrastructure and Management of Health Services in the UT of Jammu 
and Kashmir  was taken up.  
The objective of the Performance Audit is to provide a holistic view of the healthcare 
sector in the UT of J&K , i.e. a macro picture using State -level information and data and 
a micro -picture arising from detailed audit analysis/ findings on maintenance of 
infras tructure and delivery of health care services. This has been done by assessing the 
following:  
• The availability of the necessary human resources at all levels, e.g. doctors, nursing, 
paramedics, etc;  
• The availability of drugs, medicines, equipment and other consumables;  
• The availability and management of healthcare infrastructure;  
• Adequacy of the funding for healthcare;  
• The funding and spending under  various schemes of the Government of India;  

Chapter I: Introduction  
9 • The adequacy and effectiveness of the regulatory mechanisms for ensuring that 
quality healthcare services are provided in / by the public/  private healthcare 
institutions/  practitioners;  and 
• Whether Government spending on health has improved the health and well -being 
conditions of the people as per SDG -3. 
1.7 Audit Scope and Methodology  
The Performance Audit covering the period from 2016 -17 to 202 0-21 was conducted 
from  December 2021 to December 2022 and wherever feasible, the data has been 
updated up to 2021 -22. The audit scope covered the healthcare facilities (both 
Allopathic and AYUSH) at primary, secondary and tertiary levels by scrutinising the 
records of the Administrative Department, Mission Director, National Health Mission ; 
Director General  Family Welfare, Maternal and Child Health (MCH) and 
Immunisation ; three5 Directors;  Managing Director, Jammu and Kashmir Medical 
Supplies Corporation Limited (JKMSCL) ; State Drug Controller (SDC) incl uding Drug 
Testing Laboratories;  two6 Principals ; Medical Superintendents concerned ; Chief 
Medical Officers ; District AYUSH Officers and Block Medical Officers (BMOs).  
Previously Performance Audit of National Rural Health Mission, Jammu Division 
(Para graph  2.1 of Report No. 01 of the year 2017) and Performance Audit of National 
Rural Health Mission, Kashmir Division (Para graph  2.2 of Report No. 03 of the year 
2018) were published in the CAG’s Audit Report  highlighting the issues of financial 
management, infrastructure and availability of human resources . The above reports 
were not discussed in Public Accounts Co mmittee as of March 2022 . 
Selection of offices/ healthcare institutions for scrutiny of records was conducted at two 
stages. In Stage -I, six7 out of 20 Districts of UT of J&K were selected by applying the 
sampling method of Probability Proportional to Size Without Replacement (PPSWOR). 
In Stage -II, Sub -Centres (SCs), Primary Health Centres (PHCs) and AYUSH 
Dispensaries/  units were sampled by applying the method of Simple Random Sampling 
Without Replacement (SRSWOR) in respect of six selected Districts as detailed in 
Chart 1.6. In respect of tertiary level health institutions, nine out of 19 were selected 
by judgmental sampling in the capital cities of Srinagar and Jammu. Districts selected 
for field audit in UT of J&K are depicted in  Map 1.1.

of the survey has been depicted in Chapter III . 
1.8 Audit Criteria  
The PA was benchmarked against criteria  derived from the following sources:  
• Indian Public Health Standards, 2012;  
• National Health Policy, 2017;  
• Sustainable Development Goals;  
• Professional Conduct, Etiquette and Ethics Regulation, 2002;  
• Drugs and Cosmetics Act, 1940;  
• Bio-Medical  Waste Management Rules, 2016;  
• National AYUSH  Mission;  
• Atomic Energy (Radiation Protection) Rules, 2004;  
• National Accreditation Board for Hospitals;  
• Assessors Guidebook for Quality Assurance;  
• Clinical Establishment Act;  
• Medical Council of India  (MCI) Act, 1956 replaced by National Medical 
 Commission Act,  2019;  
• NITI Aayog Reports;  
• Orders/instructions/reports issued by the GoI/  State/  UT Government from time 
 to time; and  
• Jammu and Kashmir (J&K) Financial Rules, General Financial Rules and Book 
 of Financial Powers.  
1.9 Consideration of Ayushman Bharat  in this Report  
Ayushman Bharat (AB), the flagship health scheme of the Government of India,  was 
launched in September 2018 to achieve Universal Health Coverage as recommended in 
the National Health Policy, 2017. AB adopts a continuum of care approach, comprising 
two inter -related components which are  detailed in Table 1.2 . 
 
 

Audit Report on Public Health Infrastructure & Management of Health Services for the period  ended March 2022  
12 Table  1.2: Components of Ayushman Bharat  
Health and Wellness Centres 
(HWCs)  • Transform existing 1,50,000 Sub -Centres and Primary Health 
Centres into HWCs.  
• Delivery of Comprehensive Primary Health Care (CPHC) 
including free essential drugs and diagnostic services covering 
maternal and child health services and non -communicable 
diseases.  
• Operationalise 12,500 Health and Wellness Centres under 
AYUSH in a phased manner during 2019 -20 to 2023 -24.  
Pradhan Mantri Jan Arogya 
Yojana (PM -JAY)  • Provides a cover of ` 5 lakh per family per year for secondary 
and tertiary care hospitalisation in public and private empaneled 
hospitals in India.  
• Over 10.74 crore poor and vulnerable families (approximately 
50 crore beneficiaries) are eligible for these benefits.  
• Provides cashless access to health care services for the 
beneficiary at the point of service, that is, the hospital.  
• Services include approximately 1,393 procedures covering all 
the costs related to treatment, including but not limited to drugs, 
supplies, diagnostic services, physician’s fees, room charges, 
surgeon charges, OT and ICU charges etc.  
• In order to provide the benefits of AB PM -JAY to all residents 
of Jammu and Kashmir (more than 20 lakh families), a scheme 
by the name of AB PM -JAY SEHAT was launched on  
26 December 2020.  
The State Health Agency (SHA), a society registered under the J&K Societies 
Registration Act, 1941 has been implementing PMJAY  for poor and vulnerable families 
since 2018. After the launch of PM -JAY SEHAT  in December 20 20, the scheme is 
being implemented by the SHA for all beneficiaries of Jammu and Kashmir under  
PM-JAY SEHAT.  In the UT of J&K, 2079 Public and Private Hospitals were empaneled 
with AB -PM-JAY/  AB- PM-JAY SEHAT as on January 2023.  
As per the Beneficiary Identification System10 (BIS) data, there were 102.45 lakh 
beneficiaries under PM -JAY/  PM-JAY SEHAT under 25.72 lakh families as of March 
2022. In order to avail the benefit s under  PM-JAY/  PM-JAY SEHAT, these 
beneficiaries were required to get themselves registered under these schemes . Out of 
102.45 lakh beneficiaries, 66.11 lakh (65 per cent ) beneficiaries were registered  under 
PM-JAY/  PM-JAY SEHAT  as on  31 March 2022 . Coverage of beneficiaries across 
districts is detailed in Table 1.3 .

Human Resource s 
 
2.1 Introduction   
The health workforce can be defined as “all people engaged in actions whose prima ry 
intent is to enhance health” . The health  workforce includes staff that provide  health 
services such as doctors, nurses  etc. as well as administrative and support staff that do 
not deliver services directly but are essential  to the performance of the health systems . 
Availability of manpower and related issues have been discussed in the succeeding 
paragr aphs. 
2.2 Human Resource s in the erstwhile State/ UT  
Delivery  of quality  healthcare  services  in hospitals  largely  depends  on adequate  
availability of doctors,  nurses, para medical and other supporting staff.  Availability of 
human resource s (including AYUSH) in the erstwhile  State/ UT of J&K during the 
period 2016 -22 is detailed in Table  2.1.

It is evident from Maps 2.1 to 2.5  that the distribution of available manpower in the  
20 districts of Jammu and Kashmir  was not uniform as detailed below:  
• Shortfall of overall manpower was lowest (11.52 per cent ) in Kulgam District 
and highest (43.55 per cent ) in Kishtwar District.  
• It was observed that posts of doctors were lying vacant in all the districts. 
Shortfall in the category of doctors was more than 40 per cent  in nine districts1. 
It was lowest (4.26 per cent ) in Kulgam District and highest (60.86 per cent ) in 
Doda District.   
• Shortfall in the category of nursing staff was more than 40 per cent  in four 
districts2. It was lowest (10.71 per cent ) in Kishtwar District and highest (56.60 
per cent ) in Budgam District.  
• Shortfall in the category of paramedical staff was more than 40 per cent  only in 
Reasi District (4 3.03 per cent ) while it was lowest (1 4 per cent ) in Udhampur  
District.  
• Shortfall in the category of other staff was more than 40 per cent  in four districts3. 
It was lowest ( 7.78 per cent ) in Kulgam District and highest (5 2.19 per cent ) in 
Kupwara District.

2.3 Doctor  Population R atio 
As per 2011 Census, population of UT of Jammu and Kashmir was 1,22,67,0134. World 
Health  Organisation (WHO) prescribes norm of one doctor per thousand population . 
As of March 2022, one Government doctor5 was serving a population of 2 ,191 (2,461 
in case of allopathic doctors) in the UT of J&K . The ratio of availability of doctor was 
better than the States of Haryana (4 ,081 doctors for 2.51 crore people i.e. one allopathic 
doctor per 6,212 persons) and Punjab (one allopathic doctor for 6,390 persons).   The 
availability of Government doctors at district level was  not uniform in the selec ted six 
Districts, and it varied  from as low as one Government doctor for 1, 568 people in 
Jammu  District to as high as one Government  doctor for 4,111  people in Udhampur 
District  as depicted in Chart  2.1. 
 
 
 
4 After excluding the population of UT of Ladakh as per Census -2011.  
5  Audit examined availability of only Gove rnment doctors per thousand population.

(B) Under Ayushman Bharat scheme , ASHAs  were required to be attached with 
AYUSH Health and Wellness Centres (AH and WCs)  at the norm of one per 1, 000 
population  to ensure Prakriti14 analysis of individuals above 18 years of age, regular 
Yoga at the se Centres , referral and follow -up care of patients . Audit observ ed that 
against  
 317 approved AH and WCs, ASHAs were  attached with only 141 AH and WCs as of 
March 2022.  
• Yoga Instructors were t o be engaged on part -time basis in all AH  and WCs. 
Against the requirement of 317  Yoga Instructors for AH  and WCs, the D epartment had 
deployed (March  2022) only 216 Yoga  Instructors . 
The Chief Accounts Officer, Directorate of AYUSH, J&K stated (January 2025) that 
in 523 AH and WCs, services of 2 ,401 ASHAs are being utilised. It was further stated 
that 20 Yoga Instructors have left the job and presently 196 Yoga Instructors are 
working. However, Yoga trainings have been imparted to Medical Officers and 
Pharmacists/  support staff for ensuring availability of Yoga services in all AH  and WCs.

2.10 Norms for allocation of Human Resources

doctors, nurses, paramedical staff and support staff to be available in different Hospitals 
from DHs up to the Sub -Centre level according to the number of  sanctioned  beds.

facility.

2.12.2  Non-sanctioning of specialist post of doctors in SDH Marh  
Audit noticed that  PHC Marh was upgraded (August 2002) to S DH. There were eight 
Casualty Doctors/  General Duty Medical Officers  but no posts of specialist doctors 
were sanctioned even after lapse of more than 19 years as of March 202 2. During the 
period 2016 -22, 2 ,95,195 patients visited the SDH Marh for treatment.  Non-sanctioning 
of specialist post of doctors  resulted  in deprival of specialised healthcare  facilities to 
the local population.

2.16  Non-availability of staff in Trauma Care Centres  
Accidental trauma is considered as a leading cause of mortality and morbidity in India. 
An accepted strategy of Trauma  Care Centre ( TCC)  is that if basic life support, first aid 
and placement of fluids can be arranged within the first hour of injury, lives of many of 
the accident victims can be saved.  As per Digest of Statistics 2020 -21, Go J&K , 699 
people died  and 5 ,951 people sustained injuries in 4 ,903 road accidents during the year 
2020  in the UT . Out of these, 106 persons18 died and 725  persons19 were  injured  in the 
sampled Districts  of Udhampur and Ramban which recorded  553 accidents.  
Ministry of Health and Family Welfare, GoI signed  a Memorandum of Understanding 
(MoU) with the GoJ&K for upgradation and strengthening of emergency facilities of 
seven hospitals in the State/ UT  including one each in the sampled DHs of Udhampur 
and Ramban for providing Trauma Care Services.  Audit observed that :  
• The TCC  with necessary equipment was established  (February 2 016) in DH 
Udhampur  at a cost of ₹ 4.58 crore20 but it  could not be made functional due to  
non-availability  of technical staff and doctors despite sanctioning of manpower  
(34 posts)  for TCC Udhampur.  Non-availability of manpower had not only 
resulted in idle investment of ₹ 4.58 crore on construction of TCC but also 
deprived the patients of envisaged facilities.  
  
Picture 2.2: Idle machinery and 
equipment in Trauma Centre Udhampur  Picture 2.3: Non-functional OT in Trauma 
Centre Udhampur  
The Medical Superintendent, Associated Hospital GMC, Udhampur stated (January 
2025) that the Trauma Care Centre could not be made functional due to non -availability 
of staff and space.  
 
18  Udhampur: 60 and Ramban: 46  
19  Udhampur: 487 and Ramban: 238  
20  Civil works: ₹ 0.90 crore; equipment: ₹ 3.68 crore  


Audit Report on Public Health Infrastructure & Management of Health Services for the period  ended March 2022  
38 • Sanction was accorded (October 2019) for creation of 34 posts for Trauma 
Centre Ramban established in the old building of DH Ramban. However, as of 
November 2023, agai nst the sanctioned strength of 35  (Specialist doctors: eight , 
MOs :six, Nurses: seven and Other staff: 14) staff , only six (Consultant 
Paediatric: one, MOs:  three , Nurse:  one and Other staff: one ) staff were posted. 
Some equipment  were received and were functional. However, i n absence of 
required staff, the Trauma Care Centre was being used as Maternal ward, 
Dialysis unit, District Early Intervention Centre (DEIC) unit and operation 
theatre .  
2.17 Conclusion  
Human resources, an essential resource for health management, witnessed persistent 
shortages in all the cadres in the health institutions. Government had not ensured 
availability of posts in the health sector considering that the sanctioned posts were not 
in keeping with the population to be served , thereby  adversely affecting health services. 
This shortage was quite high in several key posts such as Doctors, Staff Nurses, 
Paramedics , etc. who  play a very important role in delivering comprehensive healthcar e 
to the beneficiaries. Moreover, available manpower including the crucial manpower has 
not been distributed uniformly across the districts.  
2.18 Recommendations  
In light of the audit findings, GoJ&K may consider:  
• Revising the sanctioned strength of Health Department at par with the IPHS 
norms, posting of staff in health institutions at par with the sanctioned 
strength in the primary, secondary and tertiary healthcare institutions. 
Government should bring out a long -term strategy and policy to reduce 
variations in doctor -population ratio across districts; and  
• Allocating  human resources in the health institutions  throughout the UT in 
a un iform manner in order to minimis e skewed distribution of manpower 
across the districts.  Availability of infrastructure and other crucial 
components should also be considered during such rationalis ation.

3.1 Outpatient D epartment

comprehensive and optimal expert care to the community and to achieve and maintain 
an acceptable standard of quality  of care . As per IPHS norms , Essential  (Minimum 
Assured Services) / Desirable (which should be aspired to be achieved)  services of 
General Medicine (GM), General Surgery, Orthopaedics, Obstetrics and  Gynaecology 
(O and G), Ophthalmology, Paediatrics, ENT2, Skin and VD3, Dental etc . were  required 
to be available in the OPDs of HIs.

hours in the afternoon) are required for six days in a week at PHC level. No specific 
OPD services are prescribed in IPHS for PHCs. OPD services were available in all the 
test-checked PHCs as per physical survey.

3.1.7  Flow of patients in OPD  
To avail outpatient services  in the hospitals, outpatients first r egister at the Outdoor 
Patient D epartment (OPD). After registration, the doctors examine the patients and 
either prescribe diagnostic tests for evidence based diagnosis or prescribe drugs as per 
their own diagnosis during consultation.  
3.1.7.1 Registration of Out patients  
Registration is a process by which a patient’s name and identity are enrolled into the 
records of the hospital in order to provide services of the hospital to the patient and to 
keep a track of various services that are availed by each patient.  
Audit observed that there was no computerised system for registration and patient 
management in the test checked primary and secondary level hospitals.  
Deputy Director (HQ), Health Services Kashmir admitted (January 2023) that there was 
no computerised system for registration and patient management in primary and 
secondary level HIs.  

Chapter -III: Healthcare Services

works out to about 12 -20 patients/ hour per counter. Audit examined the number of 
patie nts registered during 2016 -22 in seven sampled TLHs, six DHs and 28 CHCs/ 
SDHs along with availability of registration counters and observed that  adequate 
number of registration counters were not available as detailed in Chart 3.1.

3.1.7.4  Prescription Audit  
Regulations 1.4 and 1.5 of the Indian Medical Council (Professional Conduct, Etiquette 
and Ethics) Regulations, 2002 provide that every physician shall display the registration 
number accorded to him/ her by the State Medical Council/ Medical Council of In dia 
in all his/ her prescriptions and should as far as possible prescribe drugs with generic 
names.  
GoJ&K accorded (February 2020) sanction for constitution of Prescription Audit 
Committees (PAC) for Secondary and Tertiary level hospitals. The PAC shall prepare 
and submit fortnightly reports to the respective Heads of Departments (HODs). 
According to the  sanction a Nodal Officer was to be nominated for collection of at least 
one per cent  of prescriptions written by the Government doctors in OPDs on daily basis 
at random. The prescriptions collected by the Nodal Officer shall be scrutinised by the 
PAC to s ee whether:  
• Prescriptions have name of doctor with registration number and are written 
legibly in capital letters over his/ her signature.  
• Generic drugs are prescribed with preference given to drugs which are available 
free in the hospital.  
• Unnecessary diagnostic tests/ procedures are prescribed, and patients are 
referred to private clinics/ specialists without requirement.  
• Drugs are prescribed in contravention to the provisions of the Drugs and 
Cosmetics Act and regulations . 
Audit observed that as of March 2022, PACs had not been constituted in any of the 
seven sampled TLHs, two7 out of six sampled DHs and 22 out of 28 sampled CHCs/ 
SDHs as depicted in Chart  3.3.

3.2.2 Evaluation of IPD services through Outcome Indicators  
The IPD services provided during 2016 -22 in the test checked H Is were evaluated 
through c ertain Outcome Indicators (OIs) such as Bed Occupancy Rate8 (BOR),  Bed 
Turnover Rate9 (BTR), Discharge Rate10 (DR), Referral Out Rate11 (ROR), Average 
Length of Stay12 (ALoS ), Leave Against Medical Advice13 (LAMA) Rate  and 
Absconding Rate14.  
 
8  BOR is an indicator of the productivity of the hospital services and is a measure of verifying whether the 
available infrastructure and processes are adequate for delivery of health services. As per IPHS, the BOR of 
hospitals should be at least 80 per cent.   
9  BTR is the rate of usage of beds in an In-patient Department in a given period and is a measure of the utilization 
of the available bed capacity and serves as an indicator of the efficiency of the hospital. High BTR indicates 
high utilisati on of the in -patient beds in a D epartment while low BTR could be due to fewer patient admissions 
or longer duration of stay in the D epartments.  
10  Discharge rate measures the number of patients leaving a hospital after receiving due health care. High 
discharge rate denotes that the hospital is providing health care facilities to the patients efficiently, on the other 
hand low rate of discharge means that the health care facilities were not adequate.  
11  ROR denote refer to higher centres that  the facilities for treatments were not available in the hospitals.  
12  ALoS is an indicator of clinical care capability and to determine effectiveness of interventions. ALoS is the 
time between the admission and discharge/death of the patient.  
13  LAMA is the term used for a patient who leaves the hospital against the advice of the doctor.  
14  Absconding Rate refers to patients who leave the hospital without informing the hospital authorities.  

Chapter -III: Healthcare Services

calculated/relevant information not provided by the concerned HIs.

of District Hospitals concerned stated (December 2023 and January 2024) that in future 
BOR shall be calculated. The Medical Superintendent, Govt. Hospital Gandhi Nagar

awaited .

place at patient care areas, availability of sufficient food and providing of food as 
per diet plan.  
Thus, due to non -conducting of patient satisfaction survey in most of the sampled HIs, 
the HI authorities were unable to develop  an effective action plan for quality 
improvement in health facilities by identify ing gaps on the basis of feedback from 
patients . 
3.2.4 Operation Theatre services  
Operation Theatre (OT) is an essential service that is to be provided to the patients. 
IPHS norms 2012 for DHs prescribe OTs for elective major surgery, emergency 
surgeries and ophthalmology/ ENT (ear, nose and throat) surgeries .

3.2.8 Intensive Care Unit  
 
3.2.8.1 Availability of ICU services  in District Hospitals  
As per IPHS 2012 norms, critically ill patients suffering from head injuries, severe 
haemorrhage, acute coronary occlusion, kidney and respiratory catastrophe, poisoning 
etc. are to be provided with lifesaving medical aid and nursing care by highly 
special ised staff in Intensive Care Unit (ICU) of District Hospital. The number of 
patients requiri ng intensive care may be about 05 to 10 per cent  of total medical and 
surgical patients in a hospital.  
Audit observed that four (Anantnag, Handwara Jammu and Udhampur) out of six DHs 
had Intensive Care Unit (ICU) and two (Ganderbal and Ramban) DHs were without 
ICU as of March 2022. However, ICU in DH Udhampur was non -functional due to 
non-availability of required manpower.   
 
28  November -2016, May -2017, April -2018, January -2020 and February -2021  

Chapter -III: Healthcare Services  
61  
ICU equipment lying idle in DH, Udhampur (27.06.2022)  
Thus, in  absence of  ICU,  patients in serious condition in need of ICU services were 
likely to be referred to other hospitals.  
The Chief Accounts Officer, Directorate of Health Services, Jammu stated  
(January 2025) that ICU in DH Udhampur is non -functional due to non -availability of 
staff and space while as ICU service is available in DH Ramban.   
3.2.8.2    Availability of essential equipment in ICU  
IPHS 2012 norms prescribe a minimum of four beds in ICU of a DH. Audit observed 
that the availability of ICU beds in the sampled DHs29 having availability of ICU 
services were more than those prescribed in the IPHS norms.   
IPHS norms also envisage each bed in the ICU to be equipped with essential equipment 
such as High -end Monitor, Ventilator, Oxygen therapy devices, Deep vein thrombosis 
prevention devices, suction and Infusion pumps. Defibrillator, Ultrasound for invasive 
procedures and Arterial Blood Gas Analysis Machine should be available in the ICU as 
common facilities . The requirement and availability of ICU equipment in three test 
checked DHs where ICUs were functional as of March 2022 are detailed in Table 3.22 .

3.2.9   Emergency  Department  
Emergency Department is the first point of contact for any critically ill patient needing 
immediate medical attention. Flow chart of Emergency Department is given in  
Chart 3.5 . 
Chart 3.5: Flow chart of Emergency Department
 
3.2.9.1 Availability  of emergency  services  in DHs and SDHs   
As per IPHS 2012 norms for DHs and SDHs, 24x7 operational emergency with 
dedicated emergency room shall be available with adequate manpower. Emergency 
shall have dedicated triage, resuscitation and observation area. Separate provision for 
examination of ra pe/ sexual assault victims should be made available in the emergency 
as per the guidelines of the Supreme Court.  


Chapter -III: Healthcare Services  
63 Emergency should have mobile X -ray, laboratory, side labs , plaster room  and minor 
OT facilities. Sufficient separate waiting areas and public amenities for patients and 
relatives should be located in such a way that these do not disturb functioning of 
emergency services . Further, procedures under emergency services required for Assault 
injuries/  Bowel  injuries/  Head  injuries/  Stab injuries/  Multiple injuries/  Perforation/  
Intestinal obstruction should be available .  
As per NHM Assessors’ Guidebook 2013, emergency protocols should be defined and 
implemented for head injury, snake bite, poisoning, drawing etc. The facility should 
have a disaster management plan in place. The status of emergency services available 
in the  sampled hospitals  is given in Table 3.23 .

failure, Left Ventricular failure, Pneumonias, meningoencephalitis, acute respiratory 
conditions, status epilepticus, burns, shock, acute dehydration etc.  Availability of care

3.2.9.3   Management of Emergency cases in PHCs  
As per IPHS 2012 norms for PHCs, 24 -hour emergency services such as appropriate 
management of injuries and accident, First Aid, stitching of wounds, incision and 
drainage of abscess, stabilisation of the condition of the patient before referral, 
treatment of dog bite/  snake bite/  scorpion bite cases and other emergency services 
should be provided in PHCs. These services are to be provided primarily by the nursing 

Chapter -III: Healthcare Services  
67 staff. However, in case of need, Medical Officer may be available to attend the 
emergencies on call basis. 24 -hour delivery services -both normal and assisted including 
appropriate and prompt referral for cases needing specialist care should be ensured.

3.2.9.4   Emergency Management of Covid -19 
Coronavirus disease (COVID -19) is an infectious disease caused by SARS -CoV -231 
virus. The COVID -19 pandemic in India was part of the worldwide pandemic  that 
started at the beginning of December 2019 . Symptoms of COVID -19 include fever, 
cough, headache, fatigue, breathing difficulties, loss of smell and taste etc. The surge 
of COVID cases put the healthcare infrastructure in the whole country including J&K 
under huge stress . Audit reviewed the emergency response of the public healthcare 
system of J&K to derive important lessons for future preparedness.  
i) COVID -19 cases in J&K  
The details of COVID -19 cases as of March 2022 are given in Table 3.27

iii) Emergency Response and Health Systems Preparedness Package (COVID 
 Package)  
The Emergency COVID Response Plan (ECRP) 2020 -21 under Emergency Response 
and Health Systems Preparedness Package (COVID Package) was approved 
(November 2020) by GoI for the UT of J&K for ₹ 171.70 crore.  The main components 
were testing and lab strengthening, procurement (excluding central supply), 
engagement of additional human resources, incentive to human resources, IEC 
activities for COVID etc.  
Out of ₹ 43.74 crore received under ECRP by the Principal, GMC, Srinagar, funds of  
₹1.84 crore were advanced to various D epartments/Associated Hospitals34 by the 
Principal, GMC, Srinagar during July 2020 and December 2021 for implementation of 
various activities . However, neither the adjustment accounts nor the utilisation 
certificates were furnished by the concerned institution in absence of which actual 
utilisation of  ₹ 1.84 crore could not be ascertained.

v) Supply of ventilators and oxygen concentrators  
 
• Ventilators  
A ventilator is a device that supports or takes over the breathing process by pumping 
air into the lungs. People including those with severe COVID -19 symptoms, who stay 
in Intensive Care Units (ICU) may need the support of a ventilator.  
During the period from March 2020 to March 2021 (March 10, 2021), 1 ,056 ventilators 
were received in the State/UT either by procurement (105) or donation (40) or under 
PM Cares (911).  The details of ventilators received in the UT of J&K during 2021 -22 
were not furnished to a udit. 219 ventilators were received in 18 out of 34 sampled 
secondary level HIs and 408 ventilators were received in seven TLHs during 2019 -22.  
 
35  Tele-ICU is a combination of telemonitoring software and hardware integration. It is a web based solution  
that will facilitate  monitoring  of vital  health   parameters  of  patients  connecting  through   web  app and  
mobile app over network to view at Nursing station and patient at TELEICU.  

Audit Report on Public Health Infrastructure & Management of Health Services for the period  ended March 2022  
70 Audit noticed that out of 219 ventilators received in 18 secondary level HIs,  
17 ventilators in four36 HIs were not being used due to non -availability of trained 
manpower, oxygen generation plant and delay in installation by the engineering staff. 
This indicated that the ventilators were issued without ascertaining the availability of 
required infrastructu re and manpower in these HIs . The audit contention is 
substantiated by the fact that 26 ventilators were shifted (May 2021) to GMC Hospital, 
Jammu from six37 secondary level HIs of Jammu District due to non -availability of 
trained manpower and oxygen plant in these HIs.  
The Block Medical Officer, Akhnoor stated (January 2025) that all the four ventilators 
are installed and functional in the new building of SDH Akhnoor.  
Out of 408 ventilators received in seven TLHs, four38 ventilators were kept  
(December 2023)  as reserve for pandemic exigencies , the rest were installed . 
  
Ventilators lying idle at SDH, Akhnoor  
(23.12. 2021) Ventilators lying unused in SDH , Ramnagar  
(20.05. 2022) 
• Oxygen Concentrators  
An oxygen concentrator (OC) is a medical grade machine which is used to deliver 
oxygen to patients with breathing related disorders. Surge in COVID -19 infection had 
resulted in an alarming increase in the number of active cases which led to spike in 
demand for OCs .  
In the six selected Districts and six39 Tertiary Level Hospitals, 5,616 Oxygen 
Concentrators (OCs) were received by the HIs by March 2022.   
Audit noticed that  out of these 5,616 OCs, 4,322 OCs were stated to be installed   
(as on March 2022)  leaving a balance of 1,294 OCs  which were yet to be put to use .

Efficient and effective Radiological and Pathological diagnostic services  are amongst 
the most  essential health care facilities for delivering quality  treatment  to the public 
based on accurate diagnosis. Many of the important Radiology and Pathology  tests were 
not performed in the sampled HIs due to lack of required equipment and skilled 
manpower. Significant  audit findings  are discussed  in the succeeding paragraphs :

disease. Availability  of adequate functional radiology equipment with consumables and 
skilled human resources are key requirements for delivery of quality radiology services.

dental X -ray, Ultrasonography, CT  Scan etc. in DHs. IPHS also prescribe diagnostic 
services under cardiac investigation, ENT, Ophthalmology, Endoscopy and Respiratory 
in DHs.

3.2.12 Maternity Services  
Maternal Mortality Rate (MMR) and Infant Mortality Rate (IMR) are important 
indicators of the quality of maternity services available in HIs. As per the Sample 
Registration System report by Registrar General of India, against MMR of 97 at the 
national leve l, MMR in UT of J&K was 77 during 2018 -20. Further, as per National 
Family Health Survey -5, IMR was 16.3 in the UT during 2019 -20 as compared to  
35.2 at national level during the year 2019 -21.  
Antenatal care (ANC), Intrapartum care (IPC) and Postnatal care (PNC) are the major 
components of maternity services.  
3.2.12.1 Antenatal  Care   
Antenatal care (ANC) is the systematic supervision of women during pregnancy to 
monitor the progress of foetal growth and to ascertain the well -being of the mother and 
the foetus.  ANC involves general and abdominal examination, laboratory 
investigations, comprehensive abortion care and management of complications such as 
Reproductive Tract Infection (RTI) and Sexually Transmitted Infection (STI).  

Audit Report on Public Health Infrastructure & Management of Health Services for the period  ended March 2022  
80 i) ANC checkup of pregnant women  
One of the major aims of safe motherhood is to register all pregnant  women before they 
attain 12  weeks of pregnancy and provide them with services such as four antenatal 
checkups.  As per the ‘Guidelines for Antenatal Care and Skilled Attendance at Birth’, 
the suggested schedule for antenatal visits is: 1st visit: within 12 weeks preferably as 
soon as pregnancy is suspected for registration of pregnancy and first antenatal  
check -up, 2nd visit: between 14 and 26 weeks, 3rd visit: between 28 and 34 weeks and 
4th visit between 36 weeks and term.  
To prevent anaemia, pregnant women need to be given one tablet of Iron Folic Acid 
(IFA: 100 mg elemental iron and 0.5 mg folic acid) every day for at least 100 days, 
starting after the first trimester (14 -16 weeks of pregnancy). IFA dose is repeated three 
months after child birth . 
Further, the administration of two doses of Tetanus Toxoid (TT) is an important step in 
the prevention of maternal and neonatal tetanus (tetanus of the newborn). The first dose 
of TT should be administered as soon as possible, preferably when the women reg ister 
for ANC and the second dose is to be given one month after the first, preferably at least 
one month before the expected date of delivery.  
Percentage of pregnant women registered and ANC, TT and IFA tablets provided to 
them in the UT of J&K as per NFHS -5 is detailed in Table 3.4 1. 
Table 3.41: Indicators of Antenatal Care, TT administration and IFA tablets in the UT of J&K  
Indicators  2019 -20

IFA (180 days) per cent  15.9 
(Source: NFHS -5 survey report)

ii) Laboratory  investigations   
Guidelines for ANC and Skilled Attendance at Birth prescribe conducting six 
laboratory investigations (Blood group including Rh44 factor, VDRL45/ RPR46, HIV47, 
Rapid Malaria, Blood sugar and HBsAg48) during visit by pregnant women at SDHs/ 
CHCs for ANC to identify pregnancy related complications . 
Audit observed that :  
• In SDH Jagti, only the facility of pathological investigation of Blood Sugar was 
available.  
• The facility of Rapid Malaria test was not available in 13 SDHs/  CHCs  
(43 per cent ) out of the sampled 28 SDHs/  CHCs  which was prescribed for 
detection of Pregnancy Malaria which can lead to cases of maternal anemia,  
premature delivery,  intrauterine  growth  retardation, and delivery of low  
birth -weight infants.  
iii)  Management  of RTI/ STI  
Reproductive Tract Infections (RTIs) including Sexually Transmitted Infections ( STIs) 
present a huge burden of disease and adversely impacts the reproductive health of 
people. The consequences of RTI/  STI can be severe and life -threatening, such as Pelvic 
Inflammatory Disease (PID), Ectopic Pregnancy and adverse pregnancy outcomes 
including abortion, stillbirth, preterm birth and congenital infections (due to Syphilis, 
HSV, Hepatitis -B virus and HIV). National Guidelines  (July 2014) on prevention, 
managemen t and control of RTI/ STI provides for 13 essential RTI/  STI drugs.  
Details of RTI/ STI patients treated in sampled DHs during 2016 -22 and shortfall in 
availability of drugs in DHs are given in Table 3.4 3:

3.2.12.2 Intra partum  care (IPC)  
Intrapartum is the time period from onset of labor to childbirth. Proper care during labor 
and childbirth saves not only mothers and their newborn babies, but also prevents 
stillbirths, neonatal deaths and other complications . 
Audit observations  on IPC have  been  discussed in  the succeeding paragraphs:  
• Preparation  of partograph  
The partograph is a tool for monitoring maternal and foetal well -being during the active 
phase of labour, and a decision -making aid when abnormalities are detected . Scrutiny 
of records of HIs revealed that out of six sampled DHs, three DHs partially used the 
partograph , whereas partographs  were not used during 2016 -22 in three DHs.  16 out of 
28 test -checked SDHs/ CHCs had not used partographs and 12 SDHs/ CHCs49 had 
either fully or partially used them.  
 
49  SDHs Sarwal, Kupwara, Langate, Ramnagar, Seer Hamdan, Bijbehara , Dooru, Kokernag, Shangus , Marh   and 
CHCs Kralpora and Kralgund  

Audit Report on Public Health Infrastructure & Management of Health Services for the period  ended March 2022  
84 i) Caesarean Section  deliveries  
A Caesarean Section (C -Section) delivery is a lifesaving surgical procedure for the 
mothers and babies when certain complications appear during pregnancy or labour . 
NHM Guidelines on “Engaging General Surgeons for performing Caesarean Sections 
and managing Obstetric Complications” envisage that around 8 -10 per cent  of total 
delivery cases require C -section .  
C-Sections conducted out of total deliveries in the selected two TLHs and six DHs are 
depicted in Chart 3.7.

(Source: Information furnished by the respective HIs)

• A high percentage of total deliveries in two50 TLHs beyond the percentage 
envisaged in NHM guidelines was conducted through C -Section.  
• During 2016 -22, out of 1,14,552 deliveries in sampled DHs, the C -section 
deliveries were 48,037 (42 per cent ). The higher percentage of C -section 
deliveries was in DH Jammu (52 per cent ) and MCCH Anantnag (55 per cent ).  
• 12 per cent  C-section deliveries out of total deliveries in DH Ramban were 
however, close to the norms.

deliveries (88,260).

national average of 14.3 per cent  as per NFHS -5 (2019 -21). The percentage of  
C-section deliveries as per NFHS -5 stood at 42.7 per cent .

3.2.13 Doctor Survey   
An interaction by Audit with 78 doctors in six DHs (16 doctors) and SDHs/ CHCs  
(62 doctors) revealed the following : 
• About 67 per cent  doctors were posted in HIs (DHs:13, SDHs/ CHCs: 39) for 
over two years.  
• Out of 78 doctors, 53 doctors were post -graduate (DHs;13, SDHs/ CHCs: 40).  
• 26 (33 per cent ) doctors (DH: 04, SDHs/  CHCs: 22) stated that the required 
infrastructure was not available in the hospital.  
• On an average one doctor was attending 67 patients per day in OPD/ IPD 
(DH:75, SDH/ CHC:59).  
• 15 doctors (DH:  01, SDHs/ CHCs:14) were not aware of medical records kept 
in the hospital.  
• 16 doctors (DHs: 05, SDHs/ CHCs: 11) stated that generic medicines were not 
available in hospital pharmacy.  
• Manual on Standard Treatment Guidelines was not available in the chambers of 
56 docto rs (DHs:  08, SDHs/ CHCs: 48).  
• 71 out of 78 doctors felt the need for improvement in the health care 
infrastructure by making equipment, machinery, drugs etc. available as per 
requirements of the patients.  
 
54  Data was taken for 2018 -19 to 2021 -22, as in 2016 -17 and 2017 -18 data was not available.  

Chapter -III: Healthcare Services

patients requiring long term/ continuous treatment.  
3.2.14 Support services  
3.2.14.1 L iquid Medical Oxygen   
Liquid Medical Oxygen is high purity oxygen used for medical treatment, and is 
developed for use in the human body. The oxygen supply is provided in hospitals 
through centralised oxygen supply, oxygen cylinders and oxygen concentrators . 
Audit observed that:  
• Centralised oxygen supply was available in four out of six selected DHs. In DH 
Handwara and DH Jammu, oxygen supply was provided through oxygen 
cylinders.  
• Out of 28 SDHs/ CHCs, centralised oxygen supply was available only in eight55 
SDHs/ CHCs . 
• There was adequate buffer stock of oxygen cylinders  and oxygen 
concentrators in all test - checked DHs and SDHs/ CHCs except CHC Batote . 
Availability of oxygen generation plants and their capacity in the sampled TLHs is 
detailed in Table 3. 48.

• The quality check of diet was being conducted in five TLHs, three DHs and in SDH 
Kupwara.  
• The quantity check of diet was being conducted in two TLHs, three DHs and in 
SDH  Kupwara.  
• Dietician was not available in five  TLHs and in none of the six sampled DHs and 
28 SDHs/  CHCs.  
In view of the above -mentioned observations, Audit could not derive an assurance that 
quality diet  was provided to patients in  the test-checked HIs.  
3.2.14.3 Laundry services  
IPHS 2012 norms provide that hospital laundry should be provided with necessary 
facilities for segregated collection, drying, pressing and storage of soiled and cleaned 
linens. It may be outsourced.   

Chapter -III: Healthcare Services  
89 Audit observed that as of March 2022, laundry service was available in five57 out of six 
test checked DHs except  DH Handwara. Out of 28 sampled SDHs/  CHCs, laundry 
service was available in only four58 SDHs.  
HIs not having laundry facility were getting linen washed by employing someone on 
call basis or sending linen to a HI having laundry facility. Lack of laundry services 
poses health risk to both staff and patients.

3.2.14.4 Ambulance Services   
IPHS 2012 norms provide for availability of 24 -hour ambulance service in DHs, SDHs 
and CHCs. It is also desirable to have an ambulance in PHCs to provide emergency 
services . 
Out of 1,263 ambulances under the control of Directorate of Hea lth Services, Jammu 
& Kashmir, 121 ambulances were o ff the road (requiring repair: 09; outlived their 
useful life: 112) and 143 ambulances were beyond economic repair as of March 2022.  
It was observed that in the sampled 06 DHs and 28 SDHs/ CHCs, ambulance service 
was available except in SDH Langate . 
Out of 52 test -checked PHCs, ambulance service was not available in 33 (63 per cent ) 
PHCs with the result the patients were deprived of ambulance service at the time of 
emergency .  
• Operationali sation of 102/108 Ambulance Service  
Toll free numbers 102 and 108 dedicated for providing ambulance service to pregnant 
women and infants and for attending medical emergency calls respectively was 
launched (March 2020) under JK Emergency Medical Service ( JKEMS) in the UT of 
Jammu and Kashmir. As of March 2023, there was a fleet of 203 Critical Care 
Ambulances (A dvance Life Support : 139; B asic Life Support : 64) and 286 Patient 
Transport  Ambulances under Janani Shishu Suraksha Karyakram (JSSK) which were 
responding to Toll free numbers 102 and 108 in the UT of J&K. Year wise work done 
by JKEMS (102 -108) Ambulance Service is detailed in Table 3.53.

3.2.14.5 Mortuary  
As per IPHS 2012 norms, mortuary provides facilities for keeping dead bodies and 
conducting autopsy . 
Audit observed that mortuary was available in five out of six sampled DHs  except DH 
Gandhi Nagar, Jammu.  Out of 28 test -checked SDHs/ CHCs, mortuary was available 
in 12 SDHs/  CHCs. However, mortuary in eight60 SDHs/  CHCs was functional and in 
four61 SDHs/  CHCs, the mortuary was non -functional due to lack of infrastructure . 
3.2.14.6 Patient Safety  
i) Infection Control  
Infection control in hospitals is vital for maintaining a safe environment for both 
patients and staff in the hospitals.  
NHM Assessor’s Guidebook requires that for cleaning and disinfection of patient care 
areas, standard practices be followed through maintenance of a checklist for hygiene 
and infection control in a hospital. Also for prevention and measurement of hospital 
associated infection, a Hospital Infection Control Committee (HICC) should be 
constituted.

March 2022  in the test checked hospitals  is detailed in Table 3. 57.

3.3 Conclusion  
The OPD services of ENT, Psychiatry, Ophthalmology , Skin and VD were not available 
in eight, nine, six and 13 DHs respectively . In 18 sampled SDHs, all the 09 essential 
OPD services were not available, and the shortfall ranged from one to 08 services. 
Similarl y, against the requirement of 06 essential OPD services, there was a shortfall 
ranging from one to five services in 10 sampled CHCs. AYUSH services were not 
availabl e in test -checked 18 SDHs and 09 CHCs (except CHC , Khour). Registration 
counters were also not found adequate in TLHs and DHs leading to long queues. PACs 
had not been framed in seven TLHs, two DHs and 22 CHCs/ SDHs.  All basic facilities 
were not available in OPD area of all SDH/  CHCs.  
All IPD services as per IPHS 2012 norms were available in three  DHs out of six 
test-checked DHs. In respect of 28 sampled SDHs/CHCs, all IPD se rvices were 
available in only seven  SDHs and one CHC. OT services were available in all  
test-checked DHs. The OT facility was not available in SDHs Jagti, Marh, Kangan, 
Seer Hamdan, Dooru, Shangus and Kokernag. Out of 52 sampled PHCs, only two PHCs 
(Majalta and Achabal) had OT facility for surgical procedures such as Vasectomy, 
Tubectomy and Hydrocelectomy. Therefore , patients had to avail these facilities in 
other HIs.  
Out of seven  sampled TLHs, average BOR was above 80 per cent in case of three  TLHs. 
While average BOR in respect of six sampled DHs and 28 CHCs/  SDHs was not 
calculated by the concerned HIs. Referral out rate in DH Ramban was higher as 
compared to other DHs. The referral out rate was more than 15 per cent in SDH 
Kupwara and CHCs Zachaldara and Banihal.  
ICU services were not available in DH Ganderbal and DH Ramban . ICU in DH 
Udhampur was non -functional due to non -availability of staff. In Emergency services, 
most of the emergency services were available in all the test -checked DHs except for 
DH Handwara. It was noticed that the availability of Emergency OT was not available 

Audit Report on Public Health Infrastructure & Management of Health Services for the period  ended March 2022  
100 in seven SDHs. Blood stor age unit was not available in 10  out of 18 SDHs. Out of 10 
sampled CHCs, treatment of Dengue Haemorrhagic fever was available only in CHC 
Banihal, treatment of Celebral Malaria was available only in three CHCs (Sohanjana, 
Khour and Banihal). Treatment of dog and snake bite cases, poisoni ng, acute 
respiratory conditions and acute dehydration was available in all the sampled CHCs. 
Out of 52 sampled PHCs, 24 -hour management of selected emergency services was 
available in 13 PHCs and em ergency on call basis, 24 -hour normal delivery services 
and referral was available 12 PHCs.  
Out of 219 ventilators received in 18 secondary level HIs, 17 ventilators in four HIs 
were not being used as of date of Audit due to non -availability of trained manpower, 
oxygen generation plant and delay in installation by the engineering staff. Out of 5,616 
OCs, 4,322 OCs were installed and 1,294 OCs were not put to use.  
Out of six  sampled DHs, all the prescribed Radio Diagnostic services as per IPHS 2012 
norms were available only in DH Anantnag. Endoscopy facility was not available in 
DH Ramban and DH Handwara. Further, facility of Barium swallow, Barium meal, 
Barium enema, IVP was  not available in DH Jammu and DH Udhampur and DH 
Ganderbal did not have facility for CT scan.  
Out of 18 sampled SDHs, all the three prescribed Radio Diagnostic services as per IPHS 
2012 norms were available in 14 SDHs. Similarly, out of 10 CHCs, all the three 
prescribed/ desirable Radio Diagnostic services as per IPHS  2012 norms were available 
in eight  CHCs. All sampled health institutions were not providing the full quota of 
prescribed pathology services, which implies that patients had to avail these services 
outside.  
In two sampled TLHs and six sampled DHs, the rate of C -Section deliveries during 
2016 -22 was beyond the percentage envisaged in NHM guidelines. Percentage of  
C-section deliveries in HIs of State/ UT (42.70 per cent ) was more than the national 
average of 14.3 per cent as per NFHS -5 (2019 -21).  
Among laundry services, the prescribed types of linen were not available in all the 
sampled HIs. The maximum shortfall of linen was in DH Anantnag whereas the 
minimum shortfall of linen was observed in DH Jammu. In respect of sampled  
28 SDHs/CHCs, availability of linen was in the range of four to 24. The maximum 
shortfall of 20 types of linen was observed in SDH Marh whereas in SDH Sogam and 
Kupwara, there was no shortfall.  
Ambulance service was available in all test -checked DHs and SDHs/CHCs except SDH 
Langate. However, ambulance service was available in only 19 out of 52 sampled 
PHCs. Out of 41 sampled HIs, seven TLHs, five DHs and eight SDHs/CHCs were 
providing diet to patients through outsourcing. Dietary services were not available in 
DH Anantnag and seven SDHs/CHCs. Dietary service was being provided by DH 
Ganderbal and 13 SDHs/CHCs to patie nts admitted under JSSK.  

Chapter -III: Healthcare Services  
101 Out of sampled 41 TLHs/  DHs/  SDHs/  CHCs, NOC from Fire and Emergency Services 
Department was obtained only in respect of three  HIs. 
3.4 Recommendations  
In the light of the audit findings, GoJ&K may consider the following:  
• Ensure availability of basic facilities and registration counters in OPDs 
of each hospital at all levels, and ensure availability of doctors as per the 
patient load in the health institutions;  
• Ensure that all OPD services, IPD services, emergency services, OT 
services, maternity services and ICU services as prescribed under IPHS 
norms for different health institutions are made available to the 
beneficiaries;  
• Ensure availability of basic diagnostic services as prescribed under IPHS 
norms at secondary level health facilities; and  
• Take steps to improve and strengthen auxiliary and support services so 
that the overall healthcare experience is improved.  

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  CHAPTER -IV 
AVAILABILITY OF DRUGS, MEDICINES, 
EQUIPMENT AND OTHER  
CONSUMABLES  

 
  

 
103 CHAPTER -IV 
 
Availability of d rugs, medicines, e quipment and other consumables  
Drugs, medicines, equipment and other consumables constitute vital components for 
delivering comprehensive health services.  With a view to relieving the service 
providers1 of the additional burden of procurement of medicines, surgical equipment, 
machinery and vehicles and their distribution to various public health institutions (HIs), 
the J&K Government approved (May 2013) establishment of Jammu and Kashmir 
Medical Supplies Corporation Limited (JKMSCL) under the administrative control of 
the Health and Medical Education Department (H&MED).  JKMSCL was incorporated 
in March 2014  as a Company under the Companies Act, 1956.  
Audit findings on various components of drug s and equipment management in the 
health institutions are discuss ed in the succeeding paragraphs.  
4.1 Availability of Essential Drugs  
Essential medicines are those that satisfy the priority health care needs of the 
population. Essential medicines are intended to be available at all times in adequate 
amounts and in appropriate dosage forms . H&MED notified (July 2018) an Essential 
Drug List (EDL) for Government H ospitals of J&K for the period 2016 -18 containing 
558 drugs (885 formulations). However , the drugs/medicines in the list were not 
categori sed for use in HIs at various levels . A revised EDL was subsequently notified 
(July 2019) containing 345 drugs (479 formulations) for its implementation in the 
Government Hospitals of Jammu and Kashmir .
