4.6 Procurement of suture items from a single supplier  
In the Report of the Comptroller and Auditor General of India on Revenue Sector and 
Public Sector Undertakings (Social, General and Economic Sectors) for the year ended 
31 March 2018, t he irregularity of extending undue benefit by JKMSCL to a single 
supplier (M/s Johnson and Johnson)  for procurem ent of suture material valuing  
` 25.48 crore during October 2017 to March 2019 rejecting the other tenderers was 
pointed out. During the current Performance Audit, it was noticed that during 2019 -21, 

Audit Report on Public Health Infrastructure & Management of Health Services for the  period  ended March 2022  
112 JKMSCL had continued to procure the suture material valuing ` 9.68 crore from the 
same supplier  (M/s Johnson and Johnson) . JKMSCL had recorded in its file noting that 
in response to NIT (2019 -20), M/s Johnson and J ohnson had quoted high rates 
regarding  130 items which seemed unreasonable and there was lack of clarity in the 
price quoted by other bidders in respect of the remaining items as to whether the price 
quote was for each box or piece. It was also mentioned in the file that there  was 
ambiguity in arr iving at the lowest bidder. Consequently , JKMSCL then requested  
M/s Johnson and Johnson to revalidate their last contract (2016 -17) to serve the dual 
purpose of procuring approved items from the firm while simultaneously according 
substantial benefit to the state exchequer. The justification on record  regarding 
procurement of suture material on the basis of the old contract is not acceptable , as 
JKMSCL should have iss ued fresh tenders to ensure fair  competitive bidding . 
The Financial Advisor/ CAO JKMSCL stated (January 2025) that the existing rate 
contract was extended under compulsion in the best interest of patient care after due 
approval of the competent authority. New rate contract for various sutures was finalised 
in November 2021 in favour of M/s Meditronics India Pvt. Ltd. and M/s Johnson & 
Johnson Ltd.  
4.7 Availability of reagents for conducting CECT Scanning  
Contrast Enhan ced Computed Tomography (CECT) s canning is  often considered the 
best method  for detecting abdominal pain,  suspected cancer, kidney or gall bladder  
stone and various infections / inflammations . In this test , intravenous contrast dye is 
ingested into the body which helps provide a detailed view of the blood vessels. It also 
helps a doctor to take a clearer and closer view of internal organs and soft tissues and 
assess the progress of treatment of any illness.  
Records of CT scanning section of two test-checked  TLHs13 of Jammu revealed that 
scanning of  1,20,083 patients was conducted during 2016 -2022 , out of which C ECT 
scanning was conducted on 24 ,482 patients.  Audit noticed  that during the period  
2016 -2021 t he reagent (contrast -dye) required for co nducting CECT scanning had not 
been  procured through JKMSCL/  open market for issue to CT/ CECT scanning section 
which also had not requisitioned the same at any stage , resulting in non -issue of 
required reagent to  20,328 patients who had undergone CECT scanning during  
2016 -21. Due to non -procurement of CEC T reagents, there was  every possibility that 
underprivileged and poor patients may have had to purchase this from the open market 
thereby increasing their out -of-pocket expenditure. During 2021 -22, out of  
4,154 patients  who underwent CECT scanning , only 14 per cent (582) patients  were 
provided the required reagent (contrast -dye). 
 
 
13  SMGS Hospital and GMC Hospital, Jammu  

Chapter IV: Availability of drugs, medicines, equipment and other consumables  
113 4.8 Store Management  
4.8.1 Physical Verification of Drug Ware houses  (DWHs)  
Rule 213 (1)  and (2)  of GFR -2017  prescribes p hysical verification  of fixed assets at 
least once in a year and recording of outcome of verification in a corresponding register.  
Audit noticed  that physical verification  of DWHs  had not been conducted regularly  by 
the Physical Verification Committee constituted by JKMSCL. During 2016 -22, 
physical verification  of the four  DWHs (Anantnag , Baramull a, Bemina and Nagrota ) 
was conducted once in June 2021 and that of three DWHs (Rajouri, D oda and Kathua) 
was conducted  in November and December  2020 . The Committee pointed out 
mismanagement of available storage space, improper maintenance of records, delay in 
verification and submission of bills without valid reasons, non -displaying of labels on 
the racks etc.  
The Financial Advisor/ CAO, JKMSCL stated (January 2025) that physical verification 
of all the regional drug warehouses has been completed upto 2024.

4.9 Non-implementation of e -Aushadhi system  
To improve drug warehousing and supply chain management in all G overnment 
Hospitals in the State, GoJ&K launched (2015) e -Aushadhi / Drug and Vaccine 
Distribution Management System (DVDMS) portal , a web based supply chain 

Audit Report on Public Health Infrastructure & Management of Health Services for the  period  ended March 2022  
114 management application at Jammu and Kashmir Medical Supplies Corporation Ltd 
(JKMSCL ). Through  the DVDMS portal, the system was to keep check on purchases, 
inventory management and distribution of various drugs and surgical  items to various 
regional and D istrict drug warehouses, DHs, CHCs, SDHs and PHCs . 
Audit noticed  that while DVDMS  had been made functional at JKMSCL level but the 
end users (SDHs/  CHCs and PHCs) were using it only for uploading requirement  of 
drugs and not for maintenance of stock and distribution of items to their sub -stores . 
Further , DVDMS was not being used for  distribution of drugs to the patients , which 
was its final objective.  
In a review meeting (23 April 2019 ), the Administrative Department decided to 
implement the DVDMS in the year 2019 by 6th May at the DH level, by 31st May at the 
SDH / CHC level  and by 30th June at PHC  (including UPHCs)  level . Audit noticed that 
as of June 2022,  the decision had not been implemented  even after lapse of over three 
years with the result that the health institutions continued to use DVDMS only for 
annual uploading of requirement and not for maintenance of stock and further 
distribution to their sub-stores.  
Implementation of DVDMS can help in reducing  cases like the  following instances of  
non-utilis ation or expiry of drugs  noticed by audit : 
• Out of 140 kits of RTI/  STI drugs received (September 20 17) in DH Ramban, 
111 kits had expired  due to non -utilisation . The Medical Superintendent, DH 
Ramban stated ( December 2023 ) that the kits were received without submission 
of specific requisition for supply of the same. The kits could not be utilised due 
to less number of patients suffering from RTI/  STI disease  reporting  at the health 
facility.  
• Out of  1,55,540 kits for five tests (Hb, blood sugar, urine protein, HIV, Rapid 
pregnancy test) proc ured by the Directorate of AYUSH  during 2019 -2022, only 
43,783 kits were utilis ed for testing, 82 ,207 kits remained unutilis ed with 
dispensaries and 29 ,550 kits expired . The Director, AYUSH J&K stated (October 
2023) that these kits could not be utilised/expired due to non -availability of 
AYUSH staff and decrease in footfall of patients during COVID -19 pandemic.  
• During 2016 -2019 , 59,800 injections/tablets whose shelf life had expired were 
declared unfit for human consumption and were destroyed in CHC Banihal . The 
Block Medical Officer, Banihal stated ( December  2023) that due to non -issue/  
issue of lesser quantity, non -existence of system for assessment of required drugs 
and identification of surplus drugs, the quantity of un -issued medicines/  drugs 
kept on accumulating over the years which resulted in expiry of shelf life.  
The Financial Advisor/ CAO, JKMSCL stated (January 2025) that there were about 
100 per cent  active users at the level of Chief Medical Officers and Block Medical 

Chapter IV: Availability of drugs, medicines, equipment and other consumables  
115 Officers whereas at District Hospital level, Medical Colleges level and at CHC/  PHC 
level, there were about 60 per cent to 80 per cent active users as of December 2024.  
4.10 Non-functional  machinery and equipment  
Audit noticed that 10 equipment14 valuing ` 0.82 crore were  lying non -functional  in 
SMGS Hospital Jammu as on March 2022  though all these  equipment were  under 
warranty and procured  during 201 7-2021 , thus rendering the expenditure of ` 0.82 crore  
incurred on procurement of these equipment  unfruitful . 
4.11 Jhelum Tawi Flood R ecovery Project  (JTFRP) -World Bank Aided Project  
JKMSCL executed agreements with suppliers/ manufacturers for supply of items worth  
` 289.69 crore sanctioned under World Bank aided p roject s. 
Records showed that out of 5 ,868 items, 68 items valuing ` 10.22 crore were not 
received (March 2022) whereas 3 ,150 items valuing ` 123.41 crore were received with 
delay ranging between three and 306 days.  
The Financial Advisor/  CAO JKMSCL J&K stated (January 2025) that against the 
purchase orders of ` 290 crore, 100 per cent supplies have been executed by the 
concerned companies.  
In addition, 24 machines/  items  valuing  ` 1.61 crore received under World Bank funded 
project and issued to Trauma Care Centre, Udhampur  had remained non -functional 
since date of receipt resulting in idle investment  of ` 1.61 crore  due to non -availability 
of staff . 
The Medical Superintendent, Associated Hospital GMC Udhampur attributed (January 
2025) non -functioning of Trauma Care Centre to non -availability of staff and adequate 
space.  
Further, i n spite of the fact that DH Ramban was already provided (January 2014) 
Laparoscopic equipment alongwith  accessories  worth  ` 30.68  lakh by the D irector 
Health Services, Jammu , two more “Full HDlcg  Fleurosense Technol ogy Lapar oscope 
Endovision Set” valuing ` 1.08 crore was supplied (January 2021) by the JKMSCL to 
the hospital. Audit noticed that DH Ramban had not project ed any such requirement. 
Further, l aparoscopic equipment  already lying with the hospital remained idle since 
June 2019  due to non -availability of trained par amedical staff. Supplying of equipment 
witho ut any requisition by the user D epartment and in absence of trained manpower 
resulted in idling of laparoscopic equipment . 
The Medical Superintendent, District Hospital Ramban stated (November 2023) that 
the laproscopic procedures have been started w.e.f. October  2022 and about 110 
 
14  Incubator, Pulse Oximeter, Radiant Warmer, Wall Monitor, Infusion Pump, C -Pap, Phototherapy Machine, 
Defibrilator, Patient Monitor and CTG machine  

Audit Report on Public Health Infrastructure & Management of Health Services for the  period  ended March 2022  
116 procedures have b een performed as of November 2023 . The machinery supplied in 
2014 is in use with ENT Department of DH Ramban.  
4.12 Conclusion  
Avai lability of all essential drugs/medicines was not ensured  in the test -checked health 
institutions. A bsence of essential drugs may have compelle d the beneficiaries to look 
and arrange for these medicines from outside. Therefore, procurement and availability 
of drugs/  medicines left a lot of scope for improvement for delivery of quality health 
care services to the masses in the UT of J&K.  Availability of prescribed essential 
equipment was also found insufficient in the test-checked institutions. Delay in 
finalis ation of rate contract would have  had adverse impact on timely supply of drugs/  
equipment to the health institutions . In the quality assurance asp ect, issues such as delay 
in empanelment of laboratories for testing and analysis of drugs and delay in furnishing 
of test/  analysis reports by empanelled  laboratories were noticed . Non -implementation 
of e-Aushadhi system for the entire chain of drug supply was also observed.  
4.13 Recommendations  
In view of the audit findings, GoJ&K may consider:  
• Taking suitable steps to address the shortfall of drugs, equipment and other 
consumables in the healthcare institutions;  
• Avoiding delay in finalis ation of rate contracts to ensure t imely supply of 
drugs/  equipment  to the health institutions ; 
• Making efforts for timely empanelment of laboratories and obtaining of test/  
analysis reports of drugs to provide quality drugs to the beneficiaries ; and 
• Utilising e -Aushadhi / DVDMS  system optimally starting from placing  of 
indents by the health institutions till final  distribution of drugs to patient.  

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
CHAPTER -V 
HEALTHCARE INFRASTRUCTURE  

 
  

 
117 CHAPTER -V 
Health care Infrastructure  
Health infrastructure  being the basic support for delivery of public health services, is 
an important indicator for understanding the health care policy and welfare 
mechanism in a State. It signifies the investment priority of the State with regard  to 
creation of health care facilities . To deliver quality health services in the public health 
facilities, adequate and properly  maintained  building  infrastructure  is of critical  
importance.  
In an effort to improve the quality of services and provide a uniform benchmark to 
assess the functionality of public health facilities, a set of standards known as the 
Indian Public Health Standards (IPHS) were first developed in the year 2007 and 
revised in 2012. These standards cover Sub -Health Centres (SHCs), Primary Health 
Centres (PHCs), Community Health Centres (CHCs), Sub -District Hospitals (SDHs) 
and District Hospitals (DHs). They provide guidance on the  infrastructure, human 
resource, drugs, diagn ostics, equipment, quality  and governance requirements for 
delivering health services at these facilities.  However, t he UT Government of Jammu 
and Kashmir has neither  adopted IPHS norms  nor framed their own norms till date.  
5.1  Requirement and availability of healthcare facilities  
As per C ensus 2011, UT of Jammu and Kashmir had a population of 1.23 crore.  
As per IPHS 2012 norms , to provide healthcare s ervices in the tribal, hilly or difficult 
areas of the  State, a Sub -Centre (SC) for approximately 3,000 population , Primary 
Health Centre (PHC) for approximately 2 0,000 population and Community Health 
Centre (CHC) for approximately 80,000 population are required. The Sub -District  
Hospital (SDH) caters to about 5 -6 lakh people. Every D istrict is expected to have a 
District H ospital.  
The availability of health institutions against requirement as of March 2022 is given 
in Table 5.1 .

5.5 Health and Wellness Centres  
The National Health Policy, 2017 recommended  strengthening the delivery of 
primary health care, through establishment of “Health and Wellness Centres (HWCs)” 
as the platform to deliv er Comprehensive Primary Healthc are and called for a 
commitment of two thirds of the health budget to primary health care.  
As per Ayushman Bhar at Comprehensive Primary Healthc are through Health and 
Wellness Centres Operational guidelines  of February 2018, the Government of India 
(GoI) announced that 1,50,000 Health & Wellness Centres (HWCs) would be created 
by transforming existing SCs and PHCs to deliver Comprehensive Primary Health care 
and declared this as one of the two components of Ayushman Bharat. The other 
component of Ayushman Bharat, namely the Pradhan Mantri Jan Arogya Yojana 
(PMJAY) aims to provide financial protec tion for secondary and tertiary care 
hospitalisation in public and private empanelled hospitals in India.  
 
6 (i) Govt. Medical College & Hospital, Jammu (ii) SMGS Hospital, Jammu (iii) Super Specialty Hospital, 
Jammu (iv) Lalla Ded Hospital, Srinagar (v) SMHS Hospital, Srinagar (vi) Super Specialty Hospital, 
Srinagar and (vii) G.B.  Pant Hospital, Srinagar.

(ii) Shortage of human resources

Ayushman Bharat Comprehensive Primary Healthcare through Health and Wellness 
Centres  (February 2018)  requires that there should be at  least three service providers 
(one Mid -Level Health Provider (Community Health Officer ) and at l east two  
Multi -Purpose Workers [MPW ]) in each upgraded SC. In addition, a team of 
Accr edited Social Health Activist  (ASHA) workers (at the norm of one ASHA 
worker per 1,000 patients) is to be provided.  
It was noticed that out of 865 HWCs in six sampled Districts, Mid -Level Health 
Providers (MLHP) were available in 617 HWCs whereas 248 HWCs lacks the 
availability of MLHP.  At least two  MPW s were not  available in 381 HWCs . ASHA

Ayushman Bharat G uidelines  provide an indicative list of eight types of required 
diagnostic services. During scrutiny of records, it was noticed that the required 
diagnostic services viz. Haemoglobin, Urine Pregnancy Rapid Test, Urine Di pstick -
Urine albumin and S ugar and Blood Glucose were available  in all 865 HWCs of the 
six sampled D istricts. However, Malaria Smear, Rapid  Diagnostic Test Kit for 
Dengue, Sickle Cell Rapid Test and collection of sputum samples were not available 
in 482, 561, 441 and 05 HWCs respectively.  
The Chief Medical Officers Anantnag  and Kupwara  attributed  (December 2023)  the 
reason for  non-availability of diagnostic services to non -prevalence of Dengue and 
Malaria in Anantnag and Kupwara D istricts . The Chief Medical Officer Ganderbal , 
Ramban and Jammu admitted non -availability of facility of diagnostic services  in the 
HWCs .

The AYUSH system advocates  holistic wellness approach aiming at prevention of 
diseases and promotion of health and well -being. GoI has granted approval (March 
2020) to operationalise 12,500 AYUSH Health and Wellness Centres (AH &WCs) 
through States/  UTs in Centrally Sponsored Scheme mode and under the broad 
umbrella of National AYUSH Mission (NAM)  by upgrading the already functional 
AYUSH dispensaries or Sub-Health Centres . 
(i) Conversion of AYUSH dispensaries to AYUSH Health and Wellness Centres  
Out of 571 AYUSH dispensaries in the UT of J&K, 317  dispensaries were upgraded 
during 2019 -22 to AH &WCs . In the six sampled Districts, out of 198 AYUSH 
dispensaries, 104 AYU SH dispensaries  were  upgraded to AH& WCs.

5.6.1 Prime Minister’s Development Package  
A comprehensive Prime Minister ’s Development Package (PMDP) consisting of 63 
indiv idual projects  was announced for J&K by the Hon’ble Prime Minister of India on 
7 November 2015  with an outlay of ` 80,068 crore. One of the projects under PMDP 
was “ Stepping up of support under creation of infrastructure in the District Hospitals, 
Sub-District  Hospi tals and PHCs over a period of five  years ” having sanctioned cost 
of ` 881.04 crore being implemented by H&MED of GoJ&K .

Chapter V: Health Infrastructure  
135 ` 5.25 crore was incurred as of March 2022  on account of raising 2nd and 3rd floor s of 
Block A, B, C and 3rd slab of Block s F and G and other civil wor ks including t russ10. 
However , during the course of execution of the work, safety audit of the building was 
entrusted  (March 2020)  to IIT Jammu on the directions of the then Advisor to 
Governor during his visit to the site in April 2019 and  thereafter no further work was 
executed on the building.  IIT Jammu finally reported ( June 2021) that the building 
under structura l audit is under -designed for hospital usage and should not be directly 
used for hospital usage. This not only resulted  in unfruitful expenditure of   
` 5.25 crore , but also deprived the population of the area of the intended benefits.  
The Department state d (September  2022) that IIT Jammu had  been requested to 
suggest  latest technological interventions to make the building structurally safe to be 
used for hospital purpose.  
The Principal, GMC Anantnag stated (January 2025) that the work is yet to be  
completed.

5.8 Non-establishing of infrastructure  
➢ Creation of separate isolation ward and Swine Flu H1N1 Laboratory  
For m olecular diagnosis of Swine Flu and other epidemics to tackle the situation 
arising  due to Swine Flu virus, Ebola virus, H IV, Bird Flu etc. at Govt. Medical 
College (GMC) Jammu, a proposal  for creation of Swine Flu H1N1 Laboratory along 
with separate isolation ward was submitted (F ebruary 20 15) by the Principal, GMC 
Jammu to H&MED having total cost of ` 6.28 crore ( ` 2.98 for creation of isolation 
ward and ` 3.30 crore for Swine Flu H1N1 Laboratory). H&MED released (March 
2015) funds of ` 6.30 crore  in favour of Principal, GMC Jammu for establishment of 
H1N1 (Swine Flu) Testing Laboratory at GMC Jammu within the shortest possible 
time.  
Audit observed that o ut of ` 6.30 crore, funds of ` three  crore  (` one crore in March 
2015 and ` two crore in January 2016)  were released in favour of the executing 
agency  (PWD)  for construction of isolation ward at GMC H Jammu . The executing 
agency completed the work and submitted (May 2016) Utilisation C ertificate for 
funds  of ` three crore (including funds advanced to Mechanical Division , GMC for  
` 13.69 lakh and material at site valuing ` 39.31 lakh ). 
Further , for establishment of Swine Flu H1N1 Laboratory, the tenders invited  (2015)  
by the purchase committee could not be finalised as only one firm qualified technical 
round  of the tender. The Department approached the National Centre for Disease 
Control (NCDC) , New Delhi  and they recommended four specialised agencies o ut of 
which the D epartment had approached the three15 specialised agencies . Finally, the  
work of establishment of BSL -3 Lab at GMC Jammu was authorised  
 
15  The Kartos International, the Biosafe Lab India Private Limited and T he Techcomp (Singapore) Private 
Limited.  


Chapter V: Health Infrastructure  
145 (September 2018) to the Biosafe Lab India Private Limited at a cost of ` 343.16 lakh 
by the Principal,  GMC Jammu  and the agreement was executed (December 2018)  
with the F irm. Audit further noticed that no provision was kept in the initial estimates  
(` 6.28 crore)  for Supply, Testing, Installation and Commissioning (SITC)  of 
electromechanical16 works  for operating of BSL -3 Lab at GMCH Jammu. 
Accordingly, the Administrative Approval for execution of the work “SITC of 500 
KVA DG set with AMF panel, water softener plant, RO Plant and 1 MVA electric 
sub-station” was accorded (June 2022) at an estimated cost of ` 143.66 lakh against 
which funds of ` 75 lakh was placed (June 2022) at the disposal of Mechanical 
Engineering Department  for operationalising  BSL-3 Lab. However , the work of SITC 
of DG set and distribution panel with proper rated cables for making all 
interconnections between DG set, AMF panel and distribution panel is yet to be 
completed  (December  2023).  
The Department stated (December 2023) that all the equipment and machinery of the 
H1N1 Lab supplied by the vendor is awaiting final testing and commissioning and the 
lab can be operationalised  by the end of January 2024.  
Delay in operationalising  the laboratory  had deprived patients of the advanced 
health care facilities.  
➢ Delay in establishment of a State Cancer Institute  
For providing state of the art facilities to cancer patients and conducting research 
activities in various aspects of cancer care, the Ministry of Health & Family Welfare 
(MoH &FW), Government of India (GoI) communicated (February/ March 2014)  its 
approval for setting up of a State Cancer Institute (SCI) at GMC Jammu under the 
Centrally Sponsored Scheme -National Programme for Prevention of Cancer, 
Diabetes, Cardio Vascular Disease and Strokes (NPCDCS). Against ` 120 crore 
proposed by the Principal GMC Jammu, MoH &FW approved (March 2018)  grant -in-
aid of ` 104 c rore (civil works: ` 32 crore and equipment: ` 72 crore) and an amount 
of ` 16 crore was to be contributed by GoJ&K as matching State share.  
Audit noticed that H&MED released (January 2020 and January 2021) Central share 
of ` 30.06 crore17 to the Principal GMC for construction of the SCI and the work was 
stipulated to be completed by December 2021.  Out of this, an expenditure of  
` 29.62 crore was incurred by the executing agency  (Chief Engineer PW (R&B) 
Department Jammu) . Audit however noticed that against the targeted date of 
completion of March 2022, 90 per cent  civil works have been completed (November 
2023) and balance work stopped by the contractor due to non -payment of bills.  
For procurement of the equipment, H&MED placed (January 2020 and January 2021) 
` 40.14 crore18, received from GoI for purchase of equipment, at the disposal of

18 January 2020 : ` 30.00 crore & January 2021: ` 10.14 crore

machine and ` 37.79 crore19 was transferred (December 2020 and January 2021) to

GMC, ` 0.54 crore was advanced (April 2021) to Accounts Officer, Board of

and balance amount of ` 1.81 crore was transferred (December 2021) to JKMSCL for 
purchase of new Tele -Cobalt machine thereby taking the total amount transferred to 
JKMSCL for purchase of equipment to  ` 39.61 crore20. The Principal, Govt. Medical 
College Jammu intimated (December 2023) that the equipment  is under procurement 
and is expected to be installed by March 2024.  
Thus , due to slow execution of works by the executing agency, establishment of SCI 
at GMC Jammu has been delayed which subsequently delayed the procurement of 
necessary equipment and consequential deprival of a dvanced healthcare and 
specialis ed treatment to cancer patients.  
➢ Regional Cancer Centre under National Cancer Control Programme

development of GMC Jammu and its Associated Hospitals as additional Regional 
Cancer Centre (RCC) under the revised National Cancer Control Programme (NCCP).  
For managing the affairs and to oversee all aspects of development of the RCC, a 
Governing Body (GB) was also constituted (July 2008) under the Chairmanship of 
Secretary, H&MED. For carrying out construction activities and purchase of 
equipment, funds of ` three  crore was placed ( April  2008) at the disposal of Principal, 
GMC , Jammu. Out of ` three  crore, an amount of ` 0.10 crore was to be utilised for 
construction activities. The RCC was also entitled to further funds of ` two crore 
which was to be released by MoH &FW after ascertaining the progress of the scheme.  
Audit observed that for about six and a half years from the date of receipt of funds 
from GoI,  no act ion was taken by the GB to oversee  development of RCC  except 
release (August 2012) of ` 10 lakh to the executing agency for civil works. Finally, a 
decision was taken (September 2014) for purchase of equipment amounting to  
` 2.90 crore. An amount of ` 2.88 crore was spent  between July 2014 and December 
2020 for procurement of equipment and on miscellaneous items including transfer of  
` 29.63  lakh to JKMSCL for procurement of p athology equipment. Howeve r, due to 
non-maintenance of relevant record s, the status of receipt of equipment from 
JKMSCL could not be ascertained.  
No further expenditure was incurred after December 2020 and ` 1.75 crore21 
(including interest of ` 1.63 crore) was lying unutilis ed (November  2023) with GMC

➢ Non-acquisition of land for construction of building  
The Controller , Drug and Food Control Organisation released (August 2015 and 
February 2016) funds of ` 0.35 crore  to the Deputy Commissioner , Pulwama for 
acquisition of land for construction of office building of Assistant Controller, Drugs 
and Assistant Commissioner, Food Safety in District Pulwama. These funds were 
transferred (March 2016) to the Collector,  Land Acquisition , Pulwama . It was noticed 
(July 2022)  by A udit that the land had not been transferred for construction of office  
building resulting in block ing of a sum of  ` 0.35 crore for over seven years.  
The Accounts Officer stated (Au gust 2022) that the matter would  be taken up with the 
Collector, Land Acquisition, Pulwama through Deputy Drug Controller, Drugs and 
Food Control Organisation, Kashmir for transfer of the land in favour of this 
organisation.  
5.9 Deficiencies in infrastructure facilities as per the Medi cal Council of 
 India  Guidelines  
Medica l Council of India (MCI), replaced  (September 2020 ) by National Medical 
Commission  (NMC) prescribed minimum standard requirements for every Medical 
College on the basis of annual MBBS admission intake capacity.  As per regulations , a 
Medical College and Associated Hospitals shall create infrastructure as per the 
minimum standards prescribed therein. Records revealed that GMC Jammu had 
annual MBBS admission intake capacity of 100 students during the period 2016 -17 to 
June 2018 . It was increased to 150 seats from June 2018. The intake capacity was 
subsequently increased (June 2019) to 180 students . Audit noticed that 20 
Departments22 were functional in GMC Jammu as per prescribed regulations . 
However , the infra structure available in various D epartments of GMC Jammu was  far 
below the minimum standards prescribed by MCI.  
The MCI minimum standard requirements for the Medical College provides for 
accommodation and infr astructure facilities for nine D epartments.  Out of nine 
Departments23 of GMC Jammu for which minimum standard requirements for 
accommodation and infrastructure we re prescribed by MCI, only two D epartments 
(Blood Bank and Biochemistry) had complied with the basic minimum requirements. 
The deficiencies in the remaining seven  Departments were far less than the minimum 
standard requirements prescribed by MCI . It was noticed that five Departments24 did 
not have basic facility of lecture halls and one Department25 did not have library . 
 
22  Excluding dentistry  
23 Anatomy, Physiology, Biochemistry, Pathology, Microbiology, Pharmacology, Forensic Medicine, 
Community Medicine and Blood Bank.  
24  Pathology, Microbiology, Pharmacology, Forensic Medicine and Community Medicine  
25  Microbiology.  

Audit Report on Public Health Infrastructure & Management of Health Services for the period  ended March 2022  
148 MCI had also conducted annual inspections of GMC Jammu for checking its 
compliance with minimum standard requirements but the reports ther eof were not 
made available to A udit due to which observations made by MCI could not be 
ascertained.  
5.10 Conclusion  
There was shortage of health institutions (Sub -Centres and CHCs) in the UT of J&K. 
Shortage of beds were noticed in PHCs, CHCs and SDHs. Requisite number of 
HWCs have been upgraded but there was acute shortage of infrastructure, availability 
of services, human resources, diagnostic services et c. in the HWCs in the selected 
Districts. Out of 415 construction works taken up for execution under NHM/  PMDP/  
Languishing  projects programme , only 177 works were completed and 238 works 
remained incomplete resulting in denial of the intended benefits to the general public.  
The building infrastructure created under District Plan/  NABARD/  BADP etc. either 
remained incomplete or unutilis ed either due to dispute or non -completion of balance 
minor works.  Out of available 170  residential quarters, only 89 quarters were allotted 
to the staff and remaining 81 quarters were either requiring  repair / renovation, 
occupied by other D epartment or not taken over by the  Health D epartment from 
executing agencies for execution of balance works. Moreover, in 52 health 
institutions, no residential quarters were available for the staff.  Further,  the Swine Flu 
H1N1 Laboratory at GMCH Jammu could not be made functional.  
5.11 Recommendations  
In light  of the audit findings, GoJ&K may consider:  
• Setting up of adequate number of health institutions viz. , SCs/  CHCs so that 
universal accessibility of healthcare is provided to all sections of s ociety;  
• Strengthening HWCs by providing requisite infrastructure, medicines, 
equipment and diagnostic services to deliver Comprehensive  Primary Health  
Care as envisaged under the operational guidelines of HWC;  
• To look into the issues of delays in commencement and completion of 
planned infrastructural works with a view to remove bottlenecks and ensure 
speedy completion ; and  
• Provision of functional residential quarters for ensuring round -the-clock 
availability of medical/  paramedical staff.

6.1 Introduction   
Health f acilities  in Jammu and Kashmir work  under the Health &  Medical Education 
Department  (H&MED) .

allotment of ` 30,513.98 crore. Expenditure on the Health sector thus accounted for 
6.97 per cent  of the total expenditure of the State/ UT.  The position of investment in

Expenditure on different functions of the Government is either  through  Revenue 
expenditure or Capital expenditure. Revenue expenditure is that which has to be 
incurred for running of administration, maintenance of assets already created or 
meeting of pensionary and debt obligations of the Government. Capital expenditure, 
on the other hand, refers to activities which are undertaken as a result of 
implementation of pre -conceived plans.

Audit Report on Public Health Infrastructure & Management of Health Services for the period  ended March 2022  
154 6.6 Funds received from GoI  
Health being a State subject, the Central Government supplements the efforts of the 
State Governments in delivery of Health  services mainly through Centrally Sponsored 
Scheme s (CSSs) such as National Health Mission (NHM), National AYUSH Mission 
(NAM), Prime Minister’s Development Package (PMDP) and Establishment of New 
Medical Colleges  etc. Besides, National AIDS Control Programme (NACP), a Central 
Sector Scheme was also being implemented in the UT of J&K.   
The percentage of funds received from GoI in comparison to the State/  UT Health 
budget ranged between 17 per cent and 32 per cent  as shown in Chart 6.7. 
Chart  6.7: Trend of percentage of State / UT Health budget received  from GoI  
(` in crore)  
 
The percentage of funds received from GoI  declined from 25 per cent  in 2016 -17 to 
17 per cent  in 2021 -22. 
The Director Finance, H& MED, J&K stated (December 2023) that in the interest of 
better patient care, developmental projects of non -recurring nature were approved 
under various Centrally Sponsored Schemes leading to increase in receipts from GoI. 
However, during the year 2021 -22, less funds were received by the Department  
resulting in decline in the receipts.  
6.6.1 Utilisation of funds under NHM  
Among the CSSs , National Health Mission (NHM) is the flagship scheme  of GoI to 
improve the overall H ealth  status of the country by providing universal access to 
equitable,  affordable, and quality health care services that are accountable and 
responsive to people’s needs.  
The funding pattern under the Mission was in the ratio of 90:10  between Go I and 
GoJ&K . The fund flow from GoI  to Health Institutions (HIs)  is as following : 2016-17 2017-18 2018-19 2019-20 2020-21 2021-22

ascertain the actual amount remitted to the SNA. The Financial Advisor and CAO,

and CAO, NHM, J&K stated (December 2023) that the expenditure was made 
on the approved activities out of the committed unspent balance of the last 
financial year(s).  
6.6.1.2  Delay in rel ease of funds received under NHM  
Funds of ` 2,410.86 crore released by the GoI, Ministry of Health and Family 
Welfare (MoH &FW) to GoJ&K  during 2016 -17 to 2020 -21 were not released by the 
Finance Department to State Health Society  within the stipulated period of 15 days. 
Delay in release of funds ranged between 10  days and 615 days . 
The Financial Advisor and CAO, NHM, J&K stated (July 2022) that after fulfilment 
of procedural formalities and availability of liquidity with the Finance Department, 
State Health Society receive s the NHM funds from the J&K Government. However, 
some time s the Ministry of Health and  Family Welfare, GoI  releases funds to the 
State Health Society at the fag end of the financial year  due to which  GoJ&K would 
not be able to release the funds during the financial year for which it was sanctioned.  
6.6.1.3  Utilisation C ertificates  awaited   
For implementation of various programmes of NHM, ` 39.21  crore was  transferred 
by Principal , Government Medical College (GMC),  Jammu  and ` 5.18 crore by the 

Audit Report on Public Health Infrastructure & Management of Health Services for the period  ended March 2022  
158 Principal, GMC, Srinagar  to various HIs/ Departments5 during 2016 -2021 . However, 
detailed ac counts and U tilisation C ertificates in respect of these funds were not 
obtained (September 2023 ) from the concerned HIs/ D epartments  to ascertain whether 
funds were utilised for the intended purposes of the programmes .  
6.6.1.4  Non-utilisation  of funds under Ayushman Bharat -Health and Wellness 
 Centres  
The National Health Policy, 2017 recommended strengthe ning delivery of primary 
health care through establishment of Health and Wellness Centres (AB -HWCs)  as the 
platform to deliv er comprehensive primary healthcare. For primary health care to be 
comprehensive, it needs to span preventive , promotive, curative, rehabilitative and 
palliative  aspect of care. The GoI announced (February 2018) creation of HWCs by 
transforming existing Sub-Health Centres and Primary Health C entres.  
The position of funds available and expenditure incurred under AB-HWCs  during the 
period 2018 -22 is given in Table 6.6.

6.6.2 National AIDS Control Programme  
For checking  spread of HIV6/AIDS7, National AIDS Control Programme  (NACP)  is 
implemented in the State/ UT of J&K by J&K AIDS Control Society  (JKACS)  with 
the help of H &MED, other Government departments, Non -Government Organisations 
(NGOs), civil society etc.  The programme is 100 per cent funded by GoI.

6.6.3  National  AYUSH  Mission  
Government of India (GoI)  launched National AYUSH Mission (NAM)  during 12th 
Plan to promote AYUSH medical system in States/  UTs. Under NAM, financial 
assistance is being provided by Ministry of AYUSH (MoA) to the State/  UT 
Governments for promotion of AYUSH healthcare facilities as per proposal s made in 
the State  Annual Action Plan (SAAP) . MoA sanctions AYUSH funds on the basis of 
SAAPs submitted to it by GoJ&K which releases funds to the Directorate of A YUSH  
(DoA ) for implementation of NAM in the State/ UT . 
6.6.3.1  Receipt  and utilisation of funds under NAM  
During 2016 -22, funds of ` 94.37  crore were approved by GoI as per the SAAP. 
Keeping in view the funding pattern of 90:10, GoI released ` 83.45 crore to GoJ&K  
out of due share of ` 84.93 crore. GoJ&K  released ` 8.62 crore  to DoA out of due 
share of ` 9.44 crore.  Out of ` 83.45 crore  released  by GoI , GoJ&K  released  
` 79.85 crore  to DoA during 2016 -22.

6.6.4  Submission of wrong Utilisation Certificate  
The Ministry of Health and  Family Welfare, GoI sanctioned (January 2017)  
` 13 crore for establishment of ‘Modern Drug and Food Testing Laboratory at 
Lakhanpur, Kathua’ under CSS ‘Strengthening of State Drug Regulatory System ’. 
The amount of ` 13 crore  was released (January 2018) by H& MED at the fag end of 
the year in favour of Controller, Drug and Food Control Organisation, J&K  after a 
delay of about one year. Due to non -utilisation of t he sanctioned funds , ` 13 crore 
was revalidated  (August 2018)  by H &MED in favour of Controller, Drug and Food 
Control Organisation, J&K . Audit noticed that the latter had submitted  (January 2019) 
Utilisation C ertificate for ` 11.26 crore out of ` 13 crore to GoI well before accor d of 
sanction (March 2019) by H &MED for  advance drawal of ` 13 crore for 
establishment of ‘Modern Drug and Food Testing Laboratory at Lakhanpur, Kathua’ . 
The UC in January 2019 was furnished in anticipation of incurring of expenditure 
only to seek further funding under the project.   
The State Drugs Controller, Drug and Food Control Organisation Jammu and 
Kashmir stated (January 2025) that the latest and corrected Utilisation Certificate 
stands submitted to the competent authority in January 2024.    
6.6.5  Non-utilisation of funds  
• The funds received for various schemes/programmes were either utili sed 
partially or not utili sed at all in GMC, Jammu. The unutili sed funds were kept under 
fixed deposits for years  together with a view to earn  interest instead of utili sing these 
for medical/ patient care services . An amount of ` 8.15 crore [ including ` 1.75 crore 
pertaining to National Cancer Control Programme (NCCP) lau nched by the GoI in 
March 2008] was kept under fixed deposits as of September  2022.  The D epartment 
could not furnish details of schemes/  programmes to which the remaining ` 6.40 crore 
pertained as no r elated records were maintained.  
• In compliance to the sanction  accorded (March 2020) by the H& MED for 
acquisition of land for ‘Establishment of Medi -city at Vijaypur, District Samba ’ under 
Capex Budget 2019 -20, the Director Health Services  (DHS) , Jammu  credited   
(March 2020) ` 4.75 crore  to Deposit Head  and withdrew ( March 2020 ) ` 2.76 crore  
as advance drawal for  acquisition of 83 kanals  of land belonging to J&K Evacuees ' 
Property Department . The amount of ` 2.76 crore was kept in the current  bank 
account of DHS,  Jammu . Audit observed that due to non -acquisition of land the 
whole amount of ` 7.51 crore  (` 4.75 crore  + ` 2.76 crore ) had remained unutilised 
(January 2023).  Moreover, p arking of ` 2.76 crore outside the exchequer in bank 

Audit Report on Public Health Infrastructure & Management of Health Services for the period  ended March 2022  
162 account since March 2020 has a commensurate impact on the liquidity of the State/  
UT.  
On being pointed out by Audit, t he Director Health Services, Jammu stated  
(October 2023) that ` 2.76 crore has been transferred (May 202 3) into revenue 
account of the G overnment. It was further stated that the balance ` 4.75 crore 
remained kept in Civil Deposits in  the absence of any dire ctions from the 
Administrative D epartment for its utilisation.   
• An amount of  ` 5.21 crore  was released  (August 2015) under CSS 
‘Upgradation/Strengthening of State Nursing Services’ to DHS Jammu for 
procurement of transport, lab equipment, AV aids, books, computer lab and furniture 
for four12 ANM/  GNM schools . As these funds were not utilised during 2015 -16, the y 
were  revalidated  and again released to DHS Jammu in February 2017 . Out of  
` 5.21 crore,  an expenditure of  ` 1.08 crore was incurred by DHS, Jammu for 
procurement of lab equipm ent, furniture etc. Funds of  ` 0.36 crore were placed at the 
disposal of four13 CMOs  concerned for procurement of petty items14. The U tilisation 
Certificates,  however, were awaited (December 2023) from four CMOs. Besides , 
` 0.61 crore  was transferred to JKMSCL for procurement of lab items  and 
` 0.28 crore paid to SICOP against the bills of furniture . An amount of ` 2.68 crore 
was lying in the current bank account of DHS  Jammu  for over five years and details 
of ` 0.20 crore  could not be ascertained during audit.  Failure of the DHS  Jammu  to 
utilise available funds  of ` 2.68 crore defeated the purpose for which the funds were 
released.  
The Director  Health Services, Jammu stated (October 2023) that ` 2.40 crore  out of  
` 2.68 crore has been transferred (May 202 3) into revenue account of the G overnment 
and ` 0.28 crore retained being the liability created by CMO Kathua for purchase of 
furniture for GNM Kathua.   
The Chief Accounts Officer , Directorate of Health Services Jammu stated  
(January 2025) that ` 0.28 crore have been remitted into Govt. account in January 
2025.  
6.6.6 Non-submission of Utilisation Certificates  
JKMSCL received funds from HoDs of indenting department s as well as from 
H&MED for procurement and supply of the drugs/instrument s and machinery/ 
equipment. JKMSCL was required to furnish UCs in respect of these fu nds to the 
HoDs concerned  within 12 months of closure of the financial year in terms of Rule 
238 (1) of GFR 2017 . Audit observed that  out of ` 1,550.7 8 crore received during 
2019 -22, UCs for only  ` 762.88 crore (49 per cent ) were submitted to H&MED.  
 
12  ANM Kishtwar ( ` 84.90 lakh), ANM Ramban ( ` 84.90 lakh), GNM Jammu ( ` 175.75 lakh) and GNM 
Udhampur ( ` 175.75 lakh)  
13  Jammu, Kishtwar, Ramban and Udhampur  
14  Books, AV aids, teaching material, mannequins etc.  

Chapter VI: Financial Management  
163 UCs in respect of the funds received directly from the HoDs could not be reviewed in 
absence of complete records.  
The Financial Advisor/  CAO JKMSCL stated (January 2025) that Utilisation 
Certificates amounting to ` 922.88 crore have been submitted to the concerned 
DDOs.  
Non-submission of UCs in time indicated poor financial control.  
6.7 Conclusion  
There was shortfall in utilisation of allotted funds  ranging between three per cent  and 
26 per cent  against the budget allocated by GoJ&K on the H ealth s ector during  
2016 -17 to 2021 -22. The Government spending on  the Health sector increased from 
5.84 per cent  of total expenditure in 2016 -17 to 7.98 per cent  in 2021 -22. Expenditure 
on the healthcare  sector was highly skewed in favour of Revenue expenditure with a 
smaller  proportion of  Capital expenditure. GoJ&K  released funds to the S tate Health 
Society  with delay s. Funds were lying unutil ised outside Government account  in 
contravention of codal provisions . 
6.8 Recommendations  
In the light of the audit findings, GoJ&K may consider:  
• Ensuring timely release of funds so that they are fully utilised to ensure 
availability of quality health care infrastructure and services to the people of 
the UT;  
• Reviewing the healthcare ecosystem in the UT to identify the constraints/  
factors adversely impacting the absorptive capacity of funds and make 
concerted efforts for their resolution;  
• Releasing funds  to the State /UT Health Society well in time for effective 
utilisation of the funds in programme implementation; and  
• Ensur ing deposit of Government money  lying outside Government account 
into the Consolidated Fund of the UT for its optimum utilisation.  
• Rigorous monitoring of expenditure to ensure that funds allotted are spent 
fully for intended purposes.

7.1 Introduction  
Health being a State / UT subject, the Central Government supplements the efforts of 
the State / UT Governments in delivery of health services through various schemes of 
primary, secondary  and tertiary care. The findings with respect  to audit of 
implementation of Centrally Sponsored S chemes in the UT of J&K are discuss ed in the 
succeeding paragraphs  
7.2 National Health Mission  
Observations noticed during review of various components of NHM programme  are 
discussed in the succeeding paragraphs.  
7.2.1 Janani Suraksha Yojana (JSY)  
Janani Suraksha Yojana (JSY) is a safe motherhood  intervention under NHM being 
implemented with the objective of reducing maternal and neo -natal mortality by 
promoting institutional delivery. The scheme integrates cash assistance with delivery 
and post -delivery care.  Janani Surakhsha Yojana (JSY) provides  all pregnant women 
with a cash compensation of ` 1,400 in rural areas and ` 1,000 in urban areas .  
The position of institutional deliveries and compensation provided  to the beneficiaries  
during the period 2016 -2022 in the U T of J& K is given in Table 7 .1.

promote safe institutional deliveries .

pregnant women and sick new -born accessing public health institutions. The scheme 
envisages free and cashless services to pregnant women including normal deliveries , 
Caesarean operations and also treatment of sick newborn s (up to 30 days after birth) in 
all Government  health institutions .

7.2.4 Immunis ation  
Immuni sation Programme is one of the key interventions for protection of children 
from life threatening conditions which are preventable. Immunis ation Programme in 
India was introduced in 1978 as Expanded Programme of Immunis ation (EPI) and in 
1985 it was expanded as Universal Immunis ation Programme  (UIP) . The Immunis ation 
activities have been an important component of National Reproductive and Child 
Health Programme under NH M. Under the Programme, vaccination is provided to 
prevent 11 vaccine preventable diseases such as Diphtheria, Pertussis, Tetanus, P olio, 
Measl es, severe form of Childhood Tuberculosis , Hepatitis B , Meningitis , Pneumonia 
caused by Haemophilus I nfluenza type B , Rubella and Rotavirus Diarrh oea.  
Details of vaccine s provided under  UIP for prevention of different diseases are given 
in Table 7.5.

Haemophilis Influenza, Type B infection and Hepatitis B).

7.2.5 Family Planning

information and guidance on family planning methods and services available, to ensure 
individuals and couples lead a healthy, happy and prosperous life.  Methods of 
contraception include oral contraceptive pills, implants, injectables, intra uterine 
devices, condoms, male and female steri lisation  etc. Permanent method s of family 
planning  are female sterilis ation ( Tubectomy or Tubal ligation ) and male sterili sation  
(Vasectomy ).

7.2.6 Birth spacing  
Intrauterine devices ( IUD), oral contraceptive pills and condoms  are three main 
contraceptive methods of family planning to help birth spacing.  Audit observed the 
following:  
i) Achievement under distribution of oral contraceptive pills and condoms was 
 satisfactory  against targets fixed.  
ii) In respect of IUD, during 2016 -2019 achievement was low  against targets 
 ranging between 29  per cent  and 47 per cent. Targets in respect of IUD were 
 reduced by 68  per cent from 44,535 in 2018 -19 to 14,098 in 2020 -21, but the 
 targets could still not be fully achieved as percentage achievement ranged 
 between 57 per cent  and 88 per cent. After  targets were again increased during 
 2021 -22, achievement was mere 28 per cent . No reasons were on record for 
 reduction of targets under IUD .  
Similarly,  in five out of six selected Districts ( in Ramban District targets were not 
recorded) achievement against targets in respect of IUD w as low ranging between  
27 per cent and 53  per cent during 2016 -2022.

7.2.8 Accredited Social Health Activist s 
Accredited Social Health Activist (ASHA)  is a trained female health activist in the 
community who creates awareness on health and its  social determinants and mobilis es 
the community towards local health planning  and increased uti lisation  and 
accountability of existi ng health services. ASHA mobilis es and facilitates the 
community in accessing health and health -related services available at various health 
institutions.  As per the guidelines of ASHA issued in 2006, t here should be one ASHA 
for a population of 1,000. 
Audit observed that : 
i) Against the cumulative target of engagement of 13 ,236 ASHA workers , 12,850 
ASHA workers were in position  in the State/ UT as of March 202 2. In the six sampled 
Districts, there was 9 3 per cent  to 99 per cent availability of ASHA workers.  
ii) Capacity building of ASHA  is critical in enhancing her effectiveness and to 
equip her with necessary knowledge and skills.  About 88 per cent  ASHAs were 
provided  with the prescribed four round s of training in the UT of J&K whereas in the 
six sampled D istricts, 87 to  100 per cent ASHA s were provided the requisite training 
as of March 2022.  
Further,  ASHAs are responsible  for facilitation of ante -natal checkups and for 
accompanying the mothers to  the health institutions for treatment/  admission.  
Audit observed that  during 2016 -2022, only 54  per cent  to 75 per cent  of women who 
gave birth at health institutions were accompanied by ASHAs  whereas in  the six 
sampled D istricts the percentage ranged between 62 per cent and 87  per cent  indicating  
that the assigned responsibilities have not been effectively performed by ASHAs  
despite the fact that there were sufficient number of trained ASHAs.

7.2.11  National Tobacco Control Programme (NTCP)  
Government of India launched the National Tobacco Control Programme (NTCP) in 
the year 2007 -08, with the aim to - (i) create awareness about the harmful effects of 
tobacco consumption; (ii) reduce the production and supply of tobacco products; 
(iii) ensure effective implementation of the provisions under “The Cigarettes and Other 
Tobacco Products (Prohibition of Advertisement and Regulation of Trade and 
Commerce, Production, Supply and Distribution) Act, 2003 ” (COTPA); (iv) help the 
people quit tobacco use; and (v) facilitate implementation of strategies for prevention 
and control of tobacco advocated by WHO Framework  Convention of Tobacco 
Control.

7.3  National AIDS  and STD  Control Programme  
National AIDS and STD Control Programme (NACP) was launched  by GoI in the year 
1992 . Phase I of NACP (1992 -1999) focused on awareness generation and blood safety. 
Phase II  (1999 -2007) witnessed launch of direct interventions across the prevention -
detection -treatment continuum  with capacity building of States on programme 
management . Phase III  (2007 -2012) was the story of scale -up with programme 
management decentralised up to the D istrict level. Phase IV (2012 -2017) was a period 
of consolidation and enhanced government funding. Phase IV (Extension) of NACP 
was first approved for the period 2017 -2020 an d then extended up to 2020 -21. 
Targeted Intervention (TI) is one of the main components under NACP -IV. The 
programme is designed to reduce the rate of HIV transmission among the core group 
such as Female Sex Workers (FSWs), Men having Sex with Men (MSM), Injecting 
Drug Users (IDUs) and bridge population like migrants and truckers. The TI 
programme is being implemented through Community Based Organisations (CBOs), 
Non-Government Organisations  (NGOs) and various government D epartments. For 
implementation of the programme, funds are being p laced at the disposal of CBOs, 
NGOs and Drawing and Disbursing Officers (DDOs).  
Audit o bserved that detailed accounts/  Utilisation Certificates (UCs ) in respect of funds 
released under NACP were outstanding against CBOs/ NGOs/ DDOs as of March 2022  
as detailed in Table 7.9 .

7.4 Conclusion  
Various inconsistencies were noticed in implementation of Centrally Sponsored 
Schemes . Cash compensation under JSY was not provided to all the beneficiaries. 
There was shortage in availability of essential drugs and consumables under JSSK.  
The targets under Immunisation  were not fully achieved. Besides this, achievement 
against the fixed target for sterilisation  and spacing methods  (IUD) was significantly 
low. The achievements against the targets fixed for screening of infants in the age group 
0-6 weeks under RBSK was very poor . The targets under IEC component of NACP 
were not fully achieved.  
7.5 Recommendations  
In light of  the audit findings, GoJ&K may consider;  
• Disbursement of incentive  to all the beneficiaries  under JSY for effective 
implementation of the schem e; 
• Providing adequate essential drugs and consumables under Janani Shishu 
Suraksha Karyakram;  
• Prioritising immunisation of children of all age groups;  
• Conducting awareness programmes and holding camps to create aware ness 
amongst the population  of sterilisation and spacing methods ; and 
• Prioritising screening of infants under RBSK.   

 
  
CHAPTER -VIII  
ADEQUACY AND EFFECTIVENESS OF 
REGULATORY MECHANISMS  

 
  

 
181 CHAPTER -VIII  
Adequacy and effectiveness of r egulatory mechanism s 
National Health Accounts1 (NHA) is a tool to describe health expenditures and flow of 
funds in both Government and private sector in the country. Focus of NHA is on 
describing (1) entities2 that provide resources to spend for health goods and services in 
the health system; (2) entities3 receiving and managing funds from financing sources 
to pay for or to purchase health goods and services; (3) entities4 receiving finances to 
produce/ provide health goods and services; and (4) use of funds across various health 
care services5. 
Expenditure on health care services is incurred through the Government s ector as well 
as the private sector . However , the role of Governmen t is not limited to Government 
Hospitals but also includes regulation of  the private sector hospitals, clinics, 
pharmacies, etc.  in the healthcare sector. Therefore, the existence of a regulatory 
mechanism is important to protect healthcare consumers from health risks, provide a 
safe working environment for healthcare professionals and to ensure public health and 
welfare provided through health programmes.  
Regulatory agencies thus monitor individual and corporate healthcare practition ers and 
facilities, inform the G overnment about changes in the way the healthcare industry 
operates, ensure  that higher safety standards and local, State and federal guidelines are 
followed and attempt to improve  healthcare quality . With a view to check the adequacy 
and effectiveness of the regulatory mechanisms, implementation of the following 
acts/rules have been analysed in audit:  
• The Drugs and Cosmetic s Act, 1940  and Rules , 1945.  
• The Clinical Establishments (Registratio n and Regulation) Act, 2010  and Rules , 
2020.  
• Bio Medical Waste (BMW) Management Rules, 2016 . 
• Atomic Energy (Radiation Protection) Rules, 2004 .

2.03 per cent .

Audit Report on Public Health Infrastructure & Management of Health Services for the period  ended March 2022  
182 8.1 The Drugs and Cosmetic s Act, 1940  and Rules, 1945  
One of the main interventions of GoJ&K is to achieve its public health o bjectives  by 
ensur ing that drugs available to the public are safe, e fficacious and conform  to the 
prescribed quality standards. Drug control is the only tool to ensure availability of drugs 
of the highest quality and purity . In order to provide safe, effective and quality drugs, 
the State Drug Controller (SDC) under Drugs and Food Control Organisation  
(D&FCO) is responsible for effective implementation of  The Drugs and Cosmetics  
(D&C) Act 1940 , and D and C Rules , 1945.  
As of March 2022 , there were 14,945 retail  outlets , 7,129 wholesale drug premises and 
84 manufacturing units . In addition, two drug testing laboratories at Srinagar and 
Jammu were also functioning  in the UT of J&K.   
8.1.1 Licensing  of pharmacies  
Section 18(c ) of D &C Act , 1940 prohibits manufacture for sale or for distribution or 
sale of any drug except in accordance with the conditions of a licence issued for such 
purpose . 
Audit noticed that  during 2016 -22, 160 unlicensed drug shops / manufacturing units  
were detected during 1,01,217  inspections  conducted  by D &FCO.   
The State Drugs Controller stated (January 2025) that the matters/  complaints as 
referred to Hon’ble courts are pending adjudication at different stages vis. Summons/  
framing of charges/  under trial.  
8.1.2 Shortcomings in d rug testing  
Rule s 51 and 52 of the D &C Rules, 1945 authorise the Inspector to inspect any 
premises, take samples, send samples for testing or analysis and institute prosecution 
in respect of breaches of the Act or Rules thereunder. Audit noticed that:  
• No targets for inspections were fixed during  2016 -2022 . 
• During 2016 -22, 21,832 samples were lifted in the UT in the course of 1,01,217 
inspections and i n the six selected D istricts, 8,422 sample s were lifted during 
42,417 inspections.   
• Out of 21,832 samples lifted, 20,451  samples were received for testing during 
2016 -22 against which 20,044 (98 per cent ) samples were tested in  the two 
provincial drug testi ng laboratories and 548 (about 3 per cent ) samples were 
found ‘Not of Standard Quality (N OSQ)’. However, after the manufacturers 
filed appeal for retesting of samples, 21 samples were sent for retesting to 
Central Drug Testing laboratory  (CDTL) , Kolkata and all were declared to be  
of Standard Quality.  

Chapter VIII: Adequacy and effectiveness of regulatory mechanism  
183 On being pointed out in audit, the State Drug s Controller stated (October 2023) that the 
samples received do not include 1 ,381 samples which were referred to the Drug Testing 
Laboratories located outside UT of J&K  and t he clearance of backlog of 40 7 samples 
is a continuous process.  It was also stated  (August 2023)  that the di fference in the 
opinion of the analysts of the l aboratories of UT/  CDTL, Kolkata can be attributed to 
calibrations, instrumental errors, separation techniques as also lack of acumen to test 
special categories of drug molecules.   
The State Drugs Controller stated (January 2025) that the constraints of testing facility 
have been addressed to a larger extent by way of upgradation of two existing Drug 
Testing Laboratories and establishment (March 2024) of new ‘Modern Drug Testing 
Labo ratory’ at Kathua.  
Audit further observed that H &MED  had not devised any mechanism under  which  
samples were to be referred to SDC  for testing on a regular basis. Instead  the samples 
were lifted by SDC  for testing on random  basis or on complaint basis.   
Rule 51(5) of t he D &C Rules, 1945 empowers the Inspector to initiate prosecutions in 
respect of breaches of the Act and Rules thereunder.  
As of March 2016, 466 cases were pending in the courts. During 2016 -22, 244 
prosecutions were launched and 1 21 cases were decided by the courts.  The pending 
cases  thus increased by 2 6 per cent (589 cases) ending March 202 2. The conviction 
percentage in prosecution cases was a mere 2 3 per cent (56 cases)  during 2016 -22. In 
the six selected D istricts, the conviction percentage was only 12 per cent  (11 cases)  in 
respect of  90 prosecution  cases  during 2016 -22. 
Low percentage of  conviction indicated that the D epartment had failed to prove the 
breaches of the Act and Rules thereunder beyond all reasonable doubt.  
8.1.3 Testing of medical devices  
Medical Devices Rules , 2017 defines Medical device s as substances used for in vitro  
diagnosis and surgical dressings, surgical bandages, surgical staples, surgical sutures. 
ligatures, blood and blood component collection bag with or without anticoagulant, 
mechanical contraceptives (condoms, intrauterine devices, tubal rings), disinfectants, 
insecticides etc. For quality control and price monitoring, medi cal devices were brought 
under G overnment regulation as drugs w ith effect from April  2020 . 
Rule 18 (1) of the Medical Device Rules (MDR), 2017 notified  (January 2017)  by 
Ministry of Health and Family Welfare under Drugs and Cosmetic Act, 1940  provides 
for designation of Medical Device Testing Officer  (MDTO)  by the Central Government 
and Rule 18 (2)  provides for designation of Medical Device Officer (MDO) by the 
Central Government or, as the case may be, the State Government.  Accordingly, 
H&MED designated (April 2018)  27 Drug Inspectors as M DOs after a delay of over a 
year. The SDC, D  and FCO had taken up (June 2022) the matter for nomination of 

Audit Report on Public Health Infrastructure & Management of Health Services for the period  ended March 2022  
184 MDTOs with the Central Drugs Standard Control O rganisation and it was stated 
(August 2022) that the name(s) from the 15 notified MDTOs for the whole of India are 
to be proposed for the purpose of nomination to be appointed as MDTOs for Jammu 
and Kashmir at various Central Medical Device Testing Laboratories  (CMDTL)  with 
due approval of UT of Jammu and Kashmir. Thereafter, s ix MDTOs for six CMDTLs 
were nominate d (December 2022) by the SDC, D &FCO , J&K for issue of notification 
for UT of J&K by GoI.  However, Rule 18 (1) of the MDR, 2017 was amended  
(June 2023) and the State  Governments  were empowered to notify centrally notified 
MDTOs. Despite the matter been taken up (June 2023) with H &MED by the SDC,  
D&FCO, J&K for issue of notification of MDTOs, H &MED failed to take any action 
(September 2023). Since the notified CMDTLs  entertained samples of medical devices 
only after the State/  UT gets MDTOs working in C MDTLs  notified, the delay in  issue 
of notification f or nomination of MDTOs resulted  in non -lifting of samples by the 27 
nominated MDOs  for testing by MDTOs. There was as such lack of adequate 
regulatory control in providing quality medical devices to consumers.  
The State Drugs Controller , Drug and Food Control Organisation , Jammu and Kashmir 
stated (January 2025) that H&MED has notified (June 2024) MDTOs of six Central 
Medical Device Testing Laboratories for UT of J&K.   
8.2 Clinical Establishments Act, 2010 and Rules thereunder  
GoI repealed (August 2019) t he Jammu and Kashmir Nursing Homes and Clinical 
Establishments (Registration and Licensing) Act, 1963  by the Jammu and Kashmir 
Reorganisation Act, 2019 . The Central Act viz. The Clinical Establishment 
(Registration and Regulation) Act, 2010  was implemented in the State/ UT by issue of 
notification  (May 2020)  for Jammu and Kashmir Clinical Establishments (Registration 
and Regulation) Rules, 2020 . By the same notification  District Registering  Authorities  
(DRA)  were established  for each D istrict of J&K  headed by District Magistrate as 
Chairperson, Chief Medical Officer as Convener and three other Members.   
Prior to establishment of DRAs, the regis tering authorities were Directors, Health 
Services, Kashmir and Jammu under Jammu and Kashmir Nursing Homes and C linical 
Establishments (Registration and Licensing) Act, 1963.   
As per provision of Section 2( c) of the CEA, 2010, clinical establishment means a 
hospital, maternity home, nursing home, dispensary, clinic, sanatorium or an institution 
by whatever name called that offers services, facilities requiring diagnosis, treatment 
or care for illness, injury,  deformity, abnormalit y or pregnancy in any reco gnised 
system of medicine established and administered or maintained by any person or body 
of persons, whether incorporated or not, and shall include a clinical establishment 
owned, controlled or managed by ( a) a Governmen t or a D epartment of the 
Government, (b) a trust, whether public or private; (c) a corporation registered under a 
central, provincial or state act, whether owned by the Government; (d) a local authority; 
and (e) a single doctor.  

Chapter VIII: Adequacy and effectiveness of regulatory mechanism  
185 8.2.1 Institutional Mechanism  
The institutional mechanism including role of National Council/ State Council and 
District Registration Authority as per Clinical Establishment (Registration and 
Regulation) Act, 2010 is as under:  
National Council  
• Classify the Clinical Establishments (CEs) into different categories.  
• Develop and recommend minimum standards.  
• Collect statistics and information in respect of C Es 
State Council  
• Compile and update  the State Registers of CEs.  
• Send  monthly returns for updating the National Register  
• Hear  appeals against the orders of the authority  
District Registration Authority  
• Registration  of CEs and  renewal / cancellation of registration of CEs.  
• Inspect ion of  CEs 
• Maint enance of data for updating State Register.  
Audit observed that e ven after lapse of over two years since CEs (Registration and 
Regulation) Rules, 2020 were notified, GoJ&K had not constituted the UT Counci l as 
of November 2022 and as such functions assigned to the UT Council  remained 
unattended.  
8.2.2 Registration of CEs  
DRA is responsible for registration of CEs in a District . The details of CEs in the private 
sector in the test-checked six Districts as of  March 2022 are given in Table 8 .1.

186 The Clinical Establishment Act, 2010 provides for two types of registration

with the prescribed minimum standards.  With the implementation  (May 2020)  of the

1963 stood cancelled .  
Audit observed that:  
• No survey was conducted by the DRAs  concerned  to ascertain t he actual 
number of CEs in the District  eligible for registration . As a result , the possibility of 
CEs operating without registration could not be ruled out.  Provisional registration was 
granted after proprietors of the CEs approached the DRA on voluntary basis  for 
registration of CE . The Chief Medical Officer, Jammu admitted (January 2025) the 
audit contentions.  The Chief Medical Officer, Ramban stated (January 2025) that 
establishments not registered are being advised to get registered.  
• As of November 2022, t he system for registration of clinics had not been started 
in Anantnag D istrict . As such , the number of clinics operating in Anantnag District 
could not be ascert ained.  
• Section 22 of the Act envisage s that application for renewal of r egistration shall 
be made 30  days before expiry of validity of provisional registration . However, no 
system was in place by which provisional registration s about to expire could be 
watched by DRAs .  
• Section 33 of the Act provides for inspection of registered CEs. As of March 
2022, there were 1 ,117 registered CEs. However, no inspection was conducted by the 
sampled six DRAs except DRA Ganderbal wh o had conducted 24 inspections during 
2021 -22. No targets were fixed for inspections . Instead,  the inspections were conducted 
on random or complaint basis.  Due to non -conducting of adequate inspections, the 
violation of provisions of the Act by the registered CEs remained unnoticed.   
• The monthly reports as required under Rule 31 (1) of the CEs (Registration and 
Regulation) Rules, 2020 were not submitted by the CEs to the three6 DRAs. 
• The D RA for registration of CEs under AYUSH w as established (March 2021) 
after a delay of 10 months of notification of CEs (Registration and Regulation) Rules, 
2020 . Audit observed that as of December 2022, no CE had been registered in the six 
selected D istricts under AYUSH even after lapse of about 21 months  since the Rules 
were notified . The Director, AYUSH, J&K stated (February 2023) that the process of 
registration  of CEs ha s been started by DRAs of all Districts . The Chief Accounts 
 
6   Kupwara, Ramban and Udhampur  

Chapter VIII: Adequacy and effectiveness of regulatory mechanism  
187 Officer, Directorate of AYUSH, J&K stated (January 2025) that 206 CEs have been 
registered by District Registering Authorities.  
8.3 Fixation of rates for delivery of services by private h ealthcare 
 establishments  
Affordability of healthcare i s critical to ensure  all income groups of the population have 
access to healthcare. H&MED had accorded (February 2012) sanction for rates to be 
charged by private C Es/ Nursing Homes/  Hospitals/  Diagnostic Centres from  patients 
for treatment, diagnostic  services  and other facilities . However, the Hon’ble High Court 
of J&K in a writ petition (Kashmir Private Hospitals versus Stat e and others) stayed 
(April 2012)  the Government Order . Though  the stay was vacated (August 2021) by 
the Hon’ble High Court of Jammu & Kashmir and Ladakh  after a lapse of about nine 
years , H&MED failed to grant sanction  (November 2023)  either for the revised rates 
or for implement ing the existing rates.  This was indicative of  a lackadaisical approach 
of the D epartment in dealing with the important issue of fixation of rates , a matter 
which  closely concerns  the public .  
Absence of Government  approved rates and lack of uniformity in rates  charged by 
Healthcare E stablishments (HEs) from consumers for various healthcare services had 
left ample scope for over -charging and profiteering  by HEs . Some instances of charging 
of different rates by different CEs  for the same tests are given in Table  8.2.

8.4 Quality Certificate from NABL and NABH  
National Accreditation Board of Labor atories (NABL) certificate signifies  a ready 
means for customers to identify and select reliable testing, measurement and calibration 
services that are able to meet their needs. Accredited laboratories receive a form of 
international recognition, which allows their data and results to be mor e readily 
accepted.   
Further, National Accreditation Board for Hospitals and Healthcare Providers (NABH) 
is a constituent board of Quality Council of India, set up to establish and operate 
accreditation programme for healthcare organisations. Accreditation results in 
improved quality of care and patient safety.  
Audit noticed that:  
• H&MED had  framed (September 2012)  a time schedule for getting 11 public 
HIs8 accredited by NABH by April 2013. However, even after lapse of over nine years, 
no hospital had got accreditation from NABH . On being pointed out in audit, the 
Director Finance H &MED, J&K stated (December 2023) that no response has been 
received from the HODs.  Out of 11 HIs, Audit  took up the matter with the seven9 
sampled HIs, of which three10 replied that they had not applied for accreditation . DH 
Anantnag  had transferred an amount of ` 11.56 lakh to Quality Council of India in

Hospitals/  Nursing Homes and three Laboratories in the private sector were granted 
NABH and NABL accreditation respectively . The Chief Medical Officer Ganderbal 
and Chief Medical Officer Anantnag stated (January 2025) that accreditation under 
NABH/  NABL has not been applied as no instructions have been received from higher 
authorities whereas the CMO, Kupwara stated  (September 2023)  that none of the 
hospitals across the District  was falling under NABH/  NABL guidelines/  standards. 
The CMO Udhampur and CMO Jammu stated  (January 2025 ) that no directions have 
been received from higher authorities for obtaining the NABH and NABL 
accreditation.  The CMO Ramban stated (January 2025) that matter shall be taken afresh 
with the authorities in the light of audit observation.   
Even  though the NABH/  NABL accreditation is voluntary , the fact also  remains that  
absence of accreditation results  in lack of assurance of  high standards  of care and 
patient safety  to the public .  
The Combined Food and Drug Laboratory, Jammu  was granted Certificate of 
Accreditation  (January 2022 ) for a period of two years.  
8.5 Bio-Medical Waste Management  
The Bio -Medical Waste (BMW) Management Rules, 2016 stipulate the procedures for 
collection, handling, transportation, disposal and monitoring of BM W with clear roles 
for waste generators and Common BMW treatment facilitators  (CBMWTF ). 
8.5.1 Grant of authorisation  
In terms of Rule 10 of BMW Rules, e very occupier or operator handling BMW, 
irrespective of the quantity is required to obtain authori sation from the State Pollution 
Control Board and Pollution Control Committee, as the case may be. Audit however 
 
11   Information not furnished by the CMOs Anantnag and Ganderbal  

Audit Report on Public Health Infrastructure & Management of Health Services for the period  ended March 2022  
190 noticed that:  
• During January 2017 to December 202 2, 6,257 HIs had applied for 
authoris ation, out of which authori sation was granted to 3,689 HIs by the J&K Pollution 
Control Committee12 (JKPCC) . Reasons for non -granting of authori sation included 
incomplete documents and unsatisfactory BMW management practices. Information 
for the year 2016 was not available  with JKPCC.  
• All the seven test-checked Tertiary Level H ospitals  (TLHs) had applied for 
authoris ation for handling BMW  as of March 2021. However, none of the test -checke d 
TLHs had been granted authoris ation as of March 2024.  Out of the six selected DHs, 
only four13 DHs had applied for authori sation whereas two14 DHs had not applied for 
authoris ation. Authori sation was obtained by DH Jammu. Similarly, out of the selected  
28 SDHs/  CHCs, 14 SDHs/  CHCs had applied for authori sation whereas nine15 SDHs/  
CHCs had not applied for authori sation. The authorisation in respect of remaining five16 
SDHs was not granted due to technical issues  like revenue papers of the site and other 
observations raise d by JKPCC.  Out of 52 selected PHCs, only 10 PHCs had applied 
for authoris ation. However, the authorisation has not been granted as of March 2024.  
• There was no system by which expiry of authorisation could be watched by the 
Jammu & Kashmir Pollution Control Committee for necessary action  for their renewal . 
Audit noticed an instance where the authorisation granted in respect of SDH Bishnah 
expired in November 2020 . However, in absence of any system for watching of expired 
authori sation , the HI applied for renewal only in July 2022.  
8.5.2 Constitution of Advisory Committee  
Rule 11 of BMW Rules, 2016 provides for constitution of an Advisory Committee 
under the Chairmanship of the Health Secretary.  The Advisory Committee shall meet 
at least once in six months and review all matters related to implementation of the 
provisions of these rules. Audit observed that:  
• Advisory Committee re -constituted (July 2017) in the UT of J&K  had 
conducted  five (2018 -19: one; 2019 -20: four) meetings . Information regarding 
meetings conducted in 2016 -17, 20 17-18 and 2020 -21 was not available with JKPCC.  
The information regarding number of Advisory Committee meetings conducted during 
2016 -22 was not available with H &MED.

191 8.5.3 Constitution of District Level Monitoring Committee  
Rule 12 (4) of BMW Rules, 2016 provides that e very State Government or Union 
Territory Administration shall constitute a District Level Moni toring Committee 
(DLMC) in the D istricts under the chairmanship of the District Magistrate or Deputy 
Commissioner or Additional District Magistrate to monitor the compliance of the 
provisions of these rules in the healthcare facilities generating BMW  and in 
CBMWTFs.  DLMC had  to submit its report regularly once in six months to the 
Advisory Committee and a copy to the State Pollution Cont rol Board or Pollution 
Control C ommittee. Audit observed that:  
• DLMC was constituted (July 2017) at D istrict level to oversee the 
implementation of BMW Rules, 2016.  
• Only Ramban D istrict had submitted  report to JKPCC  in 2019 -20. No 
information was available with JKPCC for the years 2016 -17 to 2018 -19 and 2020 -21. 
8.5.4 Submission of Annual Reports  
Rule 13 of BMW Rules provides for submission of Annual R eports by every occupier 
or operator of CBMWTF  to the prescribed authority.  
Audit noticed that Annual reports had not been submitted by two17 out of seven selected 
TLHs , two18 out of six selected DHs and 1019 out of 28 selected SDHs/  CHCs to JKPCC 
during 20 16-21. SDH Jagti had also not submitted the Annual reports to JKPCC since 
its upgradation  (June 2019)  to 2020 -21.
